2.7 Public-key Encryption and the RSA System
In this section we take a small detour from our path towards designing a secure communication protocol. This allows us to introduce another basic cryptographic concept, public-key encryption (PKE), as well as the so-called RSA scheme
(more precisely, the RSA trapdoor one-way permutation), which will in Section 2.8 also serve as the basis of a digital signature scheme.

2.7.1 Concept and Definition
The concept of public-key encryption is shown in Figure 2.13. A user (here Bob)
generates a key pair consisting of a secret key (also called private key or decryption
key) sB and the corresponding public key (also called encryption key) pB , which
he makes public, for example by putting it on his web-page, authenticated by
a certificate.25 There is an encryption operation, making use of the public key
(but not the secret key) by which any entity (e.g. Alice) can encrypt a message
for Bob. Bob (and only he) can decrypt the ciphertext using the secret key.
Informally, a public-key encryption (PKE) scheme consists of three efficient algorithms:
• The key generator is a probabilistic algorithm which generates a key pair

totic family of groups and a specific binary representation of the group elements.
24 Ueli Maurer, Towards the equivalence of breaking the Diffie-Hellman protocol and comput-

ing discrete logarithms, Advances in Cryptology - CRYPTO ’94, Lecture Notes in Computer Science,
Springer-Verlag, 1994.
25 A certificate is a statement signed by a trusted party, often called a certification authority (CA),
which confirms the binding of the public key to a particular entity.

39

40

23 Note that to define hardness formally (see possibly later), one would need to define an asymp-

(consisting of a public key and a secret key) according to a certain probability distribution.

1. The challenger chooses a secret key/public key pair according to the keypair distribution and sends the public key to the adversary.

• The encryption algorithm takes as inputs a public key and a plaintext and
computes the ciphertext. Encryption can be probabilistic with fresh randomness for every message.

2. The adversary can choose up to t ciphertexts and receive (from the challenger) their decryptions under the secret key.

• The decryption algorithm takes as inputs a secret key and a ciphertext and
computes the plaintext.
More formally, we can define a public-key cryptosystem as follows:
26

Definition 2.15. A public-key encryption (PKE) scheme for message space M,
ciphertext space C, public-key space P, and secret-key space S, consists of:27
• a key-pair distribution, a probability distribution over P × S,
• a randomized28 encryption function E: M × P → C,
• a decryption function d: C × S → M ∪ {⊥},29

such that for any message m
 ∈ M and any key pair (p, s) with positive probability, Pr d(E(m, p), s) = m = 1 (correctness).

The practicality condition is that efficient algorithms for sampling from the
key-pair distribution and for encryption and decryption are given.

2.7.2 Security Definitions for PKE
Informally, security of a PKE-scheme means that it must be computationally
infeasible to compute “useful” information about the plaintext for a given ciphertext without knowledge of the secret key.
As for the case of symmetric encryption, “leaking no useful information” can
be captured by demanding that an adversary cannot distinguish the ciphertexts
of any two messages chosen by her.
One must also specify what type of attack an adversary can perform. In a
PKE-scheme, anybody can encrypt messages, hence we do not need to consider
an encryption oracle in the definition. But to achieve a high level of security, one
can allow the adversary to have access to a decryption oracle. This is summarized in the following bit-guessing problem (see Section 2.3.3), in the literature
usually called a game between the adversary and a challenger.
Definition 2.16. t-ciphertext IND-CCA game for public-key encryption:
26 also called a public-key cryptosystem (PKC)

3. The adversary chooses two messages m0 and m1 of the same length.
4. The challenger chooses a uniformly random bit b, computes the encryption c of mb , and returns c to the adversary.
5. The adversary can again choose ciphertexts and receive their decryptions,
as in step 2, where the total number of ciphertexts (including step 2) is
limited to t, and where ciphertext c is excluded.
6. The adversary guesses b (i.e., which message was encrypted) by issuing a
guess b′ .30
Definition 2.17. If in the above definition no decryption queries are allowed
(t = 0, i.e., steps 2 and 5 are omitted), then the game is called the IND-CPA game
for public-key encryption.
Informally, a PKE-scheme is called IND-CCA secure (respectively IND-CPA
secure) if every feasible (adversary) algorithm has only negligible advantage in
the respective game.
This IND-CCA security definition seems intuitively very strong, but we do
not (yet) know (nor address the question) whether and why this definition is
adequate or too strong or too weak in a certain application context. The answer
actually depends on the type of application.

2.7.3 Using Public-key Encryption
In order for a public-key cryptosystem to be applicable, the public key must be
authenticated. This is achieved by transmitting it over an authenticated channel.
Such a channel can, for instance, be obtained by the use of so-called certificates,
which will not be discussed in depth in this course.
Public-key encryption is especially useful in a context with many entities,
not just two. For example, B has published his public key authentically, and
every entity should be able to send a secret message to B. This is the typical
setting when public-key cryptography is used. It corresponds, for example, to a
setting where many entities can connect to a server whose public key is publicly
known.
Public-key encryption is used primarily for key management,31 i.e., the
transmitted (encrypted) message is a randomly chosen key, often called a ses-

sion key (or a pre-master secret), that serves as a secret key for symmetric encryption and authentication. One advantage of this approach is the efficiency:
symmetric encryption can be implemented two to four orders of magnitude
faster than public-key encryption.
Conversely, one can also use a key agreement scheme such as the DiffieHellman protocol (combined with symmetric encryption) as a public-key encryption scheme, as described below.

2.7.4 Using the Diffie-Hellman Protocol as a PKE-Scheme
The Diffie-Hellman protocol can be used as a PKE-scheme, as follows. Let the
group G and the generator g be fixed and the order |G| = q be known, and let
a secure symmetric encryption scheme (e, d) with key space K (e.g. K = {0, 1}k
for a suitable k) be given. Let γ be a function G → K such that a random element
of G is mapped by γ to an essentially random element of K.
• Key generation (by Bob): Choose xB uniformly at random from Zq . The
secret key is xB , the public key is yB = g xB .
• Encryption: Choose x uniformly at random
 from Zq . The ciphertext for a
x
message m is the pair g x , e(m, γ(yB
), r) , where r is a uniformly random
value from the randomness space of e.
• Decryption: left as an exercise.
For the special case where the OTP is used for symmetric encryption (more
specifically, the message m is assumed to be represented as a group element
and the encryption is simply multiplication in the group with the key, i.e., the
ciphertext is m · kAB ), this scheme is known as the so-called ElGamal public-key
cryptosystem.

the DDH-assumption), but that it is not IND-CCA secure.

2.7.5 Trapdoor One-way Permutations
We now discuss another approach to devising a public-key encryption scheme,
based on the following notion.
Definition 2.18. A trapdoor one-way permutation (TOWP) for domain X ,
codomain Y, parameter set P, and trapdoor set T consists of
• a parameter-trapdoor distribution, a probability distribution over P × T ,
• a function f : X × P → Y, and

• a function g : Y × T → X ∪ {⊥},
43

Figure 2.14: Mechanical analog of a trapdoor one-way permutation.

such that for all x ∈ X and for all pairs (p, t) ∈ P × T with positive probability,
g(f (x, p), t) = x (correctness).
The practicality condition is that efficient algorithms for sampling from the
parameter-trapdoor distribution and for computing f and g are given.
The security requirement is that the following TOWP inversion game must
be computationally hard, i.e., no feasible algorithm has non-negligible success
probability.
Definition 2.19. TOWP inversion game (a game in the sense of Section 2.3.1)
between an adversary and a challenger:
1. The challenger generates a pair (p, t) according to the parameter-trapdoor
distribution, chooses a uniformly random x ∈ X , and sends f (x, p) and p
to the adversary.
2. The adversary wins the game if she outputs x.
The above description of the game follows the style used in the common cryptographic literature (where typically one directly defines security of
a TOWP). In a formulation of this game in the sense of Section 2.3.1, one avoids
words like challenger and adversary and simply describes the system that internally generates (p, t) according to the parameter-trapdoor distribution, chooses
a uniformly random x ∈ X , and outputs f (x, p) and p. Then it takes an input x′
and the game is won if and only if x′ = x.
Note that the definition of the game does not exclude that partial information
about x is computed.
The mechanical analog of a TOWP is shown in Figure 2.14.

2.7.6 The RSA Trapdoor One-way Permutation
This section briefly summarizes the RSA scheme32 already discussed (in the
Computer Science curriculum) in the courses Discrete Mathematics and Informa32 R. L. Rivest, A. Shamir, and L. Adleman, A method for obtaining digital signatures and publickey cryptosystems, Communications of the ACM, Vol. 21, No. 2, pp. 120–126, 1978.

44

tion Security, where it was called a public-key encryption scheme. The connection of the RSA TOWP and the related (naı̈ve) public-key encryption scheme
will be discussed later.
To understand the RSA TOWP, we need the following simple theorem.
Theorem 2.5. Let G be some finite group (with neutral element 1), and let e ∈ Z be a
given exponent relatively prime to |G| (i.e. gcd(e, |G|) = 1). The e-th root of y ∈ G,
namely x ∈ G satisfying xe = y, can be computed according to
x = yd ,
where d is the multiplicative inverse of e modulo |G|, i.e.,
d ≡|G| e−1 .
Proof. We have

where k = (ed − 1)/|G| is an integer. The under-braced term is equal to 1 because of Lagrange’s theorem which states that the order of a subgroup of a finite
group G divides the group order |G|. In particular, the order of every element
divides |G|.
When |G| is known, then d satisfying d ≡|G| e−1 can be computed using the
extended Euclidean algorithm. No general method is known for computing e-th
roots in a group G without knowing its order. This can be exploited to define a
TOWP.
Motivated by the Diffie-Hellman protocol also based on modular exponentiation, Rivest, Shamir and Adleman suggested as a possible class of groups the
groups Z∗n , where n = pq is the product of two sufficiently large secret primes p
and q. The order of Z∗n ,
|Z∗n | = ϕ(n) = (p − 1)(q − 1),
can be computed only if the prime factors p and q of n are known.

We can hence define the RSA TOWP as follows.
Definition 2.20. RSA trapdoor one-way permutation: The parameter-trapdoor
distribution is defined by the following process: A pair of primes, p and q, is
chosen according to some distribution, let n = pq, then e with gcd e, ϕ(n) = 1
is chosen according to some distribution. The parameter is the pair p = (n, e)
and the trapdoor is t = (n, d) where d is defined by (2.3). Moreover, X is a set
contained in Zn for all possible n. Then f is defined as f (x, p) = xe in Zn , and g
is defined as g(y, t) = y d in Zn .
Note that f and g are efficiently computable. Typically, p and q are chosen
such that n has a certain bit-length (e.g. 2048 bits), and quite often p and q
are restricted to be of (roughly) equal bit-size. Often, e is chosen to be a small
constant, for example e = 3, and p and q are chosen to satisfy gcd(p − 1, e) = 1
and gcd(q − 1, e) = 1.
Let us briefly discuss the security of the RSA TOWP. It is not known whether
computing e-th roots modulo n when gcd(e, ϕ(n)) = 1 is easier than factoring n, but it is widely believed that the two problems are computationally about
equally hard.33 Factoring large integers is believed to be computationally infeasible. If no significant breakthrough in factoring is made and if processor
speeds keep improving at the same rate as they are now, then a 2048-bit modulus appears to be secure for the next 30 years. However, factoring is known to be
efficient on a quantum computer (Shor’s polynomial-time algorithm), and RSA
and other factoring-based cryptographic schemes will be insecure once sufficiently large quantum computers can be built.

2.7.7 Public-key encryption Using a TOWP
A naı̈ve way to use a TOWP as a PKE-scheme is to let the domain of the TOWP
be the message space and to encrypt simply by applying the TOWP, where the
parameter of the TOWP is the public key of the PKE-scheme and the parameter/trapdoor pair is the secret key of the PKE-scheme. In terms of mechanical
analogs, this corresponds to using the (receiver’s) padlock to lock a box, which
can then be sent over an insecure (or authenticated) channel. The receiver can
decrypt, i.e., remove the lock. If the RSA TOWP is used, the resulting naı̈ve (or
plain) version of the public-key cryptosystem is shown in Figure 2.15.
However, it is important to note that naı̈ve RSA encryption, as described
above, is deterministic and therefore insecure, for example in applications with
33 One can prove that breaking RSA by a generic algorithm (only performing operations in the ring
Zn ) is equivalent to factoring: D. Aggarwal and U. Maurer, Breaking RSA generically is equivalent
to factoring, Advances in Cryptology - EUROCRYPT 2009, LNCS, vol. 5479, pp. 36–53, 2009.

2.8.1 Concept and Definition
The concept of a digital signature scheme is shown in Figure 2.16. A user (on the
left) generates a key pair consisting of a secret signing key (or private key) and
the corresponding public signature verification key, which he makes public, for
instance by putting it on his web-page, authenticated by a certificate. Knowledge of the secret key enables one to sign messages, while the public key allows
one to verify signatures.
Informally, a digital signature scheme consists of three efficient algorithms:
• The key generator is a probabilistic algorithm which generates a key pair,
consisting of a signing key (or secret key or private key) and a signature verification key (or public key), according to a certain probability distribution.
• The signing algorithm takes as inputs a signing key and a message and
computes the signature for the message. Signing can be probabilistic.

Figure 2.15: The naı̈ve or plain RSA public-key cryptosystem. Bob’s public key
is the pair (n, e) and his secret key is d. Alice can encrypt a message m, represented as a number in Zn , by raising it to the e-th
power modulo n. Bob decrypts a ciphertext c by raising it to the
d-th power. The symbol • indicates that this communication is
authenticated.

• The signature verification algorithm takes as inputs a signature verification
key, a message, and a signature, and outputs a bit (which can be interpreted as “accept” or “reject”).
More formally, we can define a digital signature scheme as follows:
Definition 2.21. A digital signature scheme (DSS) for message space M, signature
set S, verification-key set V, and signing-key set Z, consists of:
• a key-pair distribution, a probability distribution over V × Z,

a small message space because each message in the message space could be
tested. Even if the message space is large, the scheme is still not secure. Therefore a reasonable public-key cryptosystem based on RSA is probabilistic, by introducing appropriate (per-message) randomness. We might later address the
problem of designing a suitable way of using a TOWP as a (secure) PKE-scheme.

• a signing function34 σ: M × Z → S,

• a verification function τ : M × S × V → {0, 1},

such that signatures are correctly verified with probability 1 (correctness), i.e.,
τ (m, σ(m, z), v) = 1 for all (v, z) with positive probability and for all m ∈ M.
The practicality condition is that efficient algorithms for sampling from the
key-pair distribution and for signing and verification are given.

2.8.2 Security Definition

2.8 Digital Signatures
We have seen that the Diffie-Hellman protocol is not secure unless the values
yA and yB are exchanged via authenticated channels. We can not use a MAC
scheme to guarantee the authenticity because Alice and Bob do not share a key
(yet); generating such a key is the goal of executing the protocol. But one can
use digital signatures to authenticate the messages, assuming that Alice and Bob
know each other’s signature public key. This can be achieved, for example, by
certificates.
47

Security of a DSS is defined via the following game (a game in the sense of
Section 2.3.1), where, as usual, security means that no feasible (adversary) algorithm has a non-negligible success probability. We describe the game in the
usual terminology of the cryptographic literature, and it is immediate to phrase
it as a system in the sense of Section 2.3.1.
Definition 2.22. Signature forgery game for t messages between an adversary and
a challenger:

2.8.3 An RSA-based Signature Scheme
The RSA scheme (or, more generally, any TOWP) can also be used for generating
digital signatures. One exploits the fact that anybody knowing the public key
can compute e-th powers, whereas only the owner of the secret key can compute
e-th roots.
The naı̈ve approach would hence be to define the signature z for a message
m (represented as an element of Zn ) as the e-th root of m, i.e., s = m1/e = md .
However, such a scheme would be insecure. One attack exploits the multiplicative property of RSA (namely, we have (mm′ )d = md m′d ). One can hence forge
the signature for the message mm′ (the product is modulo n) if one has obtained
the signatures for m and m′ .
To turn the RSA-TOWP into a secure signature scheme, one must use a carefully designed encoding of the message into an element of Zn . This will not be
discussed here.
Another problem is that one wants to sign long messages, not just messages
that can be represented as a number modulo n. A natural approach to encoding
messages is therefore to apply a so-called cryptographic hash function h (see
Section 2.8.4 below), to the message before applying the d-th power operation:
Alice’s signature s for a message m is

A signature z can be verified by checking the equation
se ≡n h(m).

s := (h(m))d (mod n).

This signature scheme (see Figure 2.17) is sometimes referred to as full domain
hash (FDH).
A necessary security requirement is that it must be infeasible to find two
messages m and m′ with the same hash value, i.e., such that h(m) = h(m′ ).
(Such a hash function h is so-called collision-resistant; see below). Otherwise an
attacker could generate two message hashing to the same value, have one of
them signed, and thereby also have a signature for the other message.
However, collision-resistance is not sufficient for the FDH signature scheme
to be secure. If the hash function were replaced by a purely theoretical model
of a truly random function (a so-called random oracle) then the security of the
FDH signature scheme can be proved. One talks about a security proof in the
so-called random oracle model (ROM). Unfortunately, one can show that there
cannot exist a hash function that “behaves like” a random oracle. This means
that a proof in the ROM cannot be carried over into a security proof when a
concrete hash function is used, no matter how cleverly it is designed.

49

50

2.8.4 Collision-Resistant Hash Functions
Definition 2.23. A hash function is a function h : D → R where |D| ≫ |R|,
typically D = {0, 1}∗ and R = {0, 1}k for some suitable k, e.g. k = 160.
Hash functions (of course, efficiently computable ones) have many applications in computer science, including databases. The purpose of a hash function
is to assign a (in a certain sense) unique short tag to an arbitrarily long bit-string.
In non-cryptographic applications it should be very unlikely that two different
bit-strings occurring in the application map to the same tag. In cryptography it
is important that the uniqueness is guaranteed even in presence of a malicious
adversary trying to provoke such a collision.
Definition 2.24. Collision-finding game for a hash function h between an adversary and a challenger:
1. The adversary can obtain the hash value h(x) for any argument x of his
choice (i.e., he has access to an h-hashing oracle).35
2. The adversary outputs a pair (x, x′ ) of messages. She wins the game if
x 6= x′ and h(x) = h(x′ ).
We make a few remarks about these definitions:
• Note that a typical value z in the range of h has many preimages x ∈ D
with h(x) = z. What the definition asks for is only that it be difficult to
find two such values, not that they do not exist.
• Definition 2.25 makes no sense for a fixed hash function h (even though
one sometimes finds it stated in the literature for a concrete hash function).
The reason is that a fixed instance of a computational problem cannot be
hard, only a computational problem with a large instance set can be hard.
More concretely, for a fixed h, there exists (of course) an algorithm that
outputs a collision for h, namely a collision fixed in its code.36 For this
reason one often sees the following definition.
Definition 2.25. (Informal.) A parameterized hash function family {hc : c ∈ C}
(e.g. C = {0, 1}s for some s) is collision-resistant if no feasible algorithm wins
the above collision-finding game for a uniformly random c ∈ C (given to the
algorithm) with non-negligible success probability.

some applications. These attacks reduce the number of operations needed for
finding a collision considerably below the O(2k/2 ) required for a brute-force attack. In 2015, the U.S. National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST)
has standardized a hash function under the name SHA-3 after a serious scientific competition for hash function proposals. SHA-3 is generally considered to
be secure. Note that all these hash functions are not defined as families and hence
are not collision-resistant in the sense of Definition 2.25.

2.8.5 Hash-then-Sign
There are other ways (not discussed here) for obtaining a signature scheme from
the RSA-TOWP or from other principles. We do not discuss how to design such
a scheme that is provably secure if the RSA-TOWP is secure, and we just mention that such methods appearing in standards are not provably secure.
Such a construction usually allows only to sign messages of a fixed length
(for example 256 bits). Assume hence that we are given a secure signature
scheme for fixed-length messages. In practice one wants to sign messages of
arbitrary length. Again, a natural approach to obtaining a signature scheme for
messages of arbitrary length is to simply hash the message and then to apply
the basic signature scheme to the hash value. This is known as the hash-thensign principle.
One can prove (as an exercise) that if the basic signature scheme is secure and
the hash function is collision-resistant, then the hash-then-sign principle yields a
secure signature scheme. More precisely, one can show that any algorithm for
forging signatures can either be used to find a collision for the hash function or
to find a forgery for the basic signature scheme.
Note that the full-domain hash (FDH) signature scheme for a TOWP and the
hash-then-sign principle discussed here are different concepts, despite the fact
that in both the message is first hashed. The TOWP is not by itself a signature
scheme, which is why FDH is generally not provably secure without making
additional assumptions.

2.9 Wrapping up this Chapter

35 Alternatively, since a description of h is known, this first step can also be eliminated since the
adversary can by herself compute hash values.
36 This does not mean that anybody knows such an algorithm.

In this chapter we have seen a number of fundamental cryptographic concepts
as well as some concrete schemes. We have discussed some security definitions
for these schemes. For the one-time pad, and also informally for MACs, we
have illustrated the constructive viewpoint we will be using later in this course.
The story of the chapter results in a concrete protocol for establishing a secure channel between two entities Alice and Bob, assuming Alice and Bob have
an authenticated copy of their communication partner’s signature verification
key, i.e., we assume the initial availability of single-use authenticated channels

51

52

For a hash function with k-bit output one can find collisions in time O(2k/2 ),
due to the so-called birthday paradox. Some well-known hash functions are
SHA-1 and MD5. There have been some recent impressive successes in the
cryptanalysis of these hash functions, leading to severe potential problems in

from Alice to Bob and from Bob to Alice. These channels are in practice constructed by the use of certificates.
In summary, and informally described, the protocol works as follows. We
only consider the case where a single message is sent securely, but by appropriate changes one obtains a protocol for sending an arbitrary number of messages.
1. Alice and Bob execute the Diffie-Hellman protocol, where the two
Diffie-Hellman messages are signed using the RSA-based FDH signature
scheme, for a specific hash function h. The signatures on the received messages are verified, and if the verification fails for a party, that party aborts
the protocol.
2. Alice and Bob each use the resulting key to generate two keys, using a
PRG g.
3. Alice expands the first of these keys, using the PRBG based on iterated
application of g, to obtain a key as long as the message to be transmitted.
4. Alice uses the one-time pad with the generated key to encrypt the message.
5. Alice uses a MAC scheme, with the second key from step 2, to compute a
MAC for the ciphertext.
6. Alice sends the ciphertext and the MAC to Bob over an insecure channel.
7. Bob verifies the received MAC on the received ciphertext, using the second
key. If the check fails, he aborts.
8. Bob expands the first of the two keys, using the PRBG.
9. Bob decrypts the ciphertext and outputs the message.

However, this only holds in an idealized setting where the hash function h is
replaced by a random oracle. Such a proof is called a proof in the random oracle
model.
So what remains to be done? The following questions, most of which will
be addressed in this course, are now well-motivated. Some of the questions are
concrete, and some are of a general conceptual type.
• What kind of mathematical objects are computational problems, methods
for solving them, and reductions? And how do we measure how well a
method solves a problem?
• Can the assumption that a hash function behaves like a random oracle be
made precise, and can there be a hash function with this property? We
will actually see that the answer to the latter question is negative, which
means that the random oracle model is in a sense not sound.
• How can we prove that the FDH signature scheme is secure if the underlying function (e.g. RSA) is a TOWP (and h is a random oracle)?
• Can one show that RSA is a TOWP, based on the assumption that factoring
is hard?
• How can we argue that the DDH problem is indeed hard. Which groups
must be excluded because the DDH problem is easy?
• Why is the continued PRG-expansion a secure PRBG?

• How can one construct a PRG from weaker assumptions, for example that
a certain function is hard to invert?
• Why is the CBC-MAC scheme secure if the underlying block cipher is secure, and are there better MAC schemes?

This protocol can be proved to be secure under certain assumptions. Secure
here means that it constructs a secure communication channel according to the
constructive cryptography paradigm. The assumptions are:37

• How can we develop a framework in which the individual security statements for the various schemes used can naturally be composed? In other
words, how can we provably exclude a failure of our intuition or hidden effects resulting from an unexpected interference between the various
schemes used?

• The DDH-problem is hard for the group used in the Diffie-Hellman protocol.

• Which other tasks, besides secure communication, can be solved by cryptography?

• The RSA function is a TOWP (according to Definition 2.18).
• The function g is a PRG (according to Definition 2.3).

In order to address these and many other questions, we need to proceed with
mathematical precision and rigor.

• The MAC scheme is secure (according to Definition 2.10).
Turned around, this means that any distinguisher that could distinguish the
“ideal setting” from the “real setting” could be used (by a reduction) to break
one of the above assumptions.
37 Of course, one also needs to assume that the certification authority works correctly, that the
computers on which the protocol is running are not compromised, etc.

Example 3.2. A (memoryless) source outputting (when triggered) independent

Example 3.3. The system that outputs a uniform random n-bit string (once,
upon a trigger input) is usually denoted as Un . (This is a {0, 1}n-source restricted to one query.)

Chapter 3

3.1 Introduction

Example 3.4. Consider a system which, for some domain X and some finite
range Y, works as follows. For every fresh input it replies with a fresh uniformly
random value, but it replies consistently if a previously given input is repeated.
This seems like a precise description of a system, but nevertheless there are two
quite different interpretations. First, the outputs can be thought of as being
generated on the fly, as just described. Second, if X is finite, the system can be
thought of as consisting of a (once and for all) randomly selected function table
embedded in the system.
We point out that in this example we have discussed three different objects,
which can all be understood as some kind of system, and in this chapter we want
to understand how to think mathematically about them. Do all these objects
mean the same thing or do they mean different things? We want to understand
which mathematical types are involved and how the three objects relate to each
other.

3.1.1 Reactive Systems

3.1.2 Descriptions vs. Mathematical Types

We consider reactive systems that proceed in rounds, in each round taking an input and producing an output. Essentially every kind of several-round discrete
system, in particular a cryptographic system, is of this form. More precisely,
an (X , Y)-system S takes inputs X1 , X2 , . . . (from some set X ) and generates, for
each new input Xi , an output Yi (from some set Y).1 The output Yi depends
(possibly probabilistically) on the current input Xi and on the internal state.
A system can be finite, i.e., it can stop answering inputs at a certain point. A
special case is that a system answers a fixed number q of input queries. The
behavior of a system can be deterministic or probabilistic.

When defining and studying mathematical objects, one must clearly distinguish
between the mathematical type of an object and descriptions of these objects in a
certain description language. The type is unique, but for an object there are generally many different ways to describe them. For example, a graph is a relation
(mathematical type), and possible descriptions include the adjacency matrix,
neighborhood lists, or the set of vertices and the set of edges. This leads to the
natural questions of when two descriptions are equivalent in the sense that they
describe the same mathematical object.
Some ways of describing a deterministic (X , Y)-system are, with increasing
level of formality:

Discrete Systems
In this chapter we introduce discrete systems, one of the most basic mathematical object considered in cryptography and in other areas of computer science.
We discuss the following mathematical types: deterministic discrete systems,
probabilistic discrete systems, and their behavior.

Example 3.1. A system that computes a function f : X → Y for every new
input, i.e., Yi = f (Xi ) for i ≥ 1, is an (X , Y)-system.
Definition 3.1. A Y-source is a system that takes trigger inputs (with a unary
alphabet, e.g. X = {⋄}) and produces (for each trigger input) an output in Y
with a certain distribution. A source can be memoryless or have memory.
1 It is not a relevant restriction to consider fixed input and output alphabets. This allows to model

also systems where inputs and outputs come from different alphabets for different i, as discussed
below.

55

• by a textual description of how the system operates.

• by a program (e.g. pseudo-code) with internal state that can be invoked
multiple times, each time with the next input.
• by a state space S, an initial state s0 ∈ S, a next-state function φ: X ×S → S,
and an output function ψ: X ×S → Y. This description is to be interpreted
as follows. Upon the ith input xi ∈ X , the new state is computed as
si = φ(xi , si−1 ) and the output is computed as yi = ψ(xi , si−1 ).
56

We now address the question of what the mathematical type of an (X , Y)system is. We will first consider deterministic, then probabilistic systems.

3.2 Deterministic Discrete Systems
3.2.1 Definition
Consider a deterministic (X , Y)-system with input alphabet X and output alphabet Y. For a first input x1 , the output y1 is a function of x1 . For a second
input x2 , the output y2 is a function of x1 and x2 . For a third input x3 , the output y3 is a function of x1 , x2 and x3 , and so on. These functions determine how
the system operates, independently of how the system is described internally
(e.g. the state space). The system is therefore completely characterized by the
definition of an output symbol for every input sequence in X ∗ \ {ǫ}, where the
empty string ǫ is excluded since the system gives no output before an input is
given. Such a system s can hence be understood as a function
s: X ∗ \{ǫ} → Y.
More generally, at a given point of the execution, a system generally accepts
only inputs from a subset of X . For example, at some point the system takes an
input bit (e.g. some kind of selection bit), at another point it accepts as input
a bitstring of arbitrary length (e.g. a message), and at yet another point it may
accept only a unary (trigger) input. This means that the function s is generally
only partially defined, and the input alphabet X comprises all possible input
values that can ever occur.2
Formally, a deterministic discrete (X , Y)-system is defined as follows:
Definition 3.2. A deterministic discrete (X , Y)-system s (an (X , Y)-DDS for short)
is a partial function3
s: X ∗ \{ǫ} → Y

with domain (denoted dom(s)) closed under taking prefixes. A system s is called
finite if dom(s) ⊆ X n for some n ∈ N.
The interpretation of this definition is that for inputs x1 , x2 , x3 , . . ., the outputs y1 , y2 , y3 , . . . are defined by4
y1 = s(x1 ),
y2 = s(x1 , x2 ),
y3 = s(x1 , x2 , x3 ), · · · .

where the special symbol ⊥ is not in Y.
One can think of the tree associated with a system s, where the edges are
labeled with input values and each node corresponds to an X -sequence in the
domain of s and is labeled with the output value for that input sequence. The
degree of a node corresponds to the size of the input alphabet at that point.
The input alphabet can also be empty; in this case the node is a leaf in the tree
and the system is considered to stop. The prefix-freeness condition of Definition 3.2 assures that a system can not be undefined for a certain input sequence
(x1 , . . . , xk ) but defined for an extension of (x1 , . . . , xk ). A finite system corresponds to a finite-depth tree.
If an input outside of the (currently) allowed domain is given, the system
does not “see” this, i.e., it does not change its state. Consequently, if later an
allowed input is given, it will reply as if no undefined input had been given.
An environment interacting with a system “sees” that a system gives no reply
and can, for example, ask a different query.5 This is captured in the following
definition.
Definition 3.3. Let s be an (X , Y)-DDS. The corresponding fully defined DDS,
denoted s⊥ , is the (X , Y ∪ { ⊥})-DDS defined as follows:


with x1 ), which make s undefined.6

3.2.2 Parallel Composition
Given a tuple (s1 , . . . , sn ) of systems, one can consider their parallel composition, a system denoted [s1 , . . . , sn ], where, informally, an input can be given to

subsystems are not affected by such a query. An input to the system [s1 , . . . , sn ]
is a pair (i, r) of an index7 i ∈ [n] selecting a subsystem and an input value
r ∈ Xi to the selected subsystem. A subsystem si only “sees” the inputs with
first component i. More formally we have:

One can partition the input alphabet X of a DDS into disjoint alphabets

3.3 Environments and Transcripts
An (X , Y)-system s is used in an environment e which provides the inputs
xi and receives the outputs yi of s. This results in a transcript of the form
x1 , y1 , x2 , y2 , x3 , y3 , . . ..
Hence, an environment is like a (Y, X )-system, except that it outputs the first
value x1 before receiving an input, which means that the output is defined also
for the empty input string ǫ. Moreover, the environment “sees” if the system
does not reply (because the input was undefined), i.e., ⊥ is in the input alphabet
of an environment.
Definition 3.6. A deterministic discrete environment e for an (X , Y)-DDS, a (Y, X )DDE for short, is a function
e: Y ∪ { ⊥}

→ X ∪ { ⊣},

where the symbol ⊣ means that the environment stops its execution.8
The transcript x1 , y1 , x2 , y2 , x3 , y3 , . . . is generated by alternating the invocation of e and of s. Actually, we need to consider the fully defined system s⊥ to
capture that the environment “sees” when the system s does not reply because
it is undefined:

X = X1 ∪ · · · ∪ Xn ,
and interpret the alphabets X1 , . . . , Xn as corresponding to n interfaces named
1, . . . , n. An input x ∈ Xi is interpreted as being given at interface i.
Analogously, one can understand a system with several interfaces as a DDS
(with a single interface). As an example of systems with many interfaces, recall the concept of a resource introduced informally in the previous chapter. A
resource is a system with interfaces to one or more parties.
A (deterministic) resource s with interface set I can be modeled as a DDS
with an input alphabet that is the union of mutually disjoint input alphabets for
each interface. In other words, an input to s determines both the value x and the
interface i to which the value is to be input. A natural choice is to let the input
alphabet of s consist of pairs (i, x) ∈ I × X , where the first component i ∈ I
indicates the interface to which the input is given, and the second component
x ∈ X denotes the input value (at that interface).

∗

x1 = e(ǫ),
y1 = s⊥ (x1 ),
x2 = e(y1 ),
y2 = s⊥ (x1 , x2 ),
x3 = e(y1 , y2 ),
y3 = s⊥ (x1 , x2 , x3 ), · · · .
Definition 3.7. The transcript of a system s in environment e, denoted t r(s, e),
is the (possibly infinite) sequence x1 , y1 , x2 , y2 , x3 , y3 , . . . defined, for i ≥ 1, by
xi = e(y1 , . . . , yi−1 )
and

Definition 3.5. A (deterministic) resource s with interface set I, input alphabet X
and output alphabet Y is a (I × X , Y)-DDS.

Different input alphabets for the different interfaces can be modeled as explained in the previous section. The output is understood to be given at the
same interface where the input was given (but this interpretation will be relevant only later).

If e(y1 , . . . , yi−1 ) = ⊣, the transcript ends with yi−1 .

3.4 Converters and Constructing Systems from Systems
3.4.1 Introduction
The idea of using a system to construct another (better) system is universal
and is used in many scientific disciplines. In this section we investigate this
paradigm for discrete systems.
A discrete (X , Y)-system s can be used to construct another discrete systems, say a (U, V)-system. For example, when given a system s (e.g. defined by
a program) that solves a certain computational problem, then one may want to
construct a system t that solves another computational problem. This transformation from s to t is generally called a reduction.
Another very simple example, which however involves probabilistic systems discussed only later, is a system s that outputs a fresh random bit for every
(unary) input, where one wants to construct a system t that outputs a uniform
ℓ-bit string for every (unary) input. This is trivially achieved as follows: Upon
each input, one invokes s ℓ times and outputs the concatenation of these ℓ bits.

3.4.2 Definition of Deterministic Converters
The object which transforms (or converts) an (X , Y)-system into a (U, V)-system
is called a converter. Informally, one can think of a converter, let’s call it α,9 as
a system with two sides, an outside and an inside. The inside is connected to
the given (X , Y)-system, say s, and the outside of the converter α serves as the
interface of the combined system. When an input u ∈ U is given (interpreted
as being input at the outside), the converter invokes the system at the inside a
finite number of times (zero times is allowed) and then returns an output v ∈ V
at the outside. The resulting system, denoted αs, is a (U, V)-system.
A bit more precisely, a converter α is a DDS whose input alphabet at the
beginning is U. Upon an input u ∈ U, the converter α produces an output,
either of the form (in, x) (intended as input x ∈ X for the system s connected
at the inside) or of the form (out, v) (interpreted to be output as v ∈ V at the
outside). In other words, the output alphabet is
{(out, u)|u ∈ U} ∪ {(in, y)|y ∈ Y}
and the flag in/out indicates whether the value is meant to be for the inside or
the outside. After an output of the form (in, x), the input alphabet is (a subset
of) Y, and the converter α takes an input y ∈ Y from the system it is conected
to. After an output of the form (out, v), the defined input alphabet is (a subset
of) U. This input value u ∈ U is interpreted as being given at the outside.
9 Converters will typically be denoted by Greek letters (e.g. α or β) or short acronyms in small
sans-serif font (e.g. enc or dec).

61

Definition 3.8. A deterministic discrete converter for converting an (X , Y)-DDS
into a (U, V)-DDS (or a ((U, V), (X , Y))-DDC for short) is a


U ∪ (Y ∪ { ⊥}), {out} × V ∪ {in} × X -DDS

whose input alphabet at the beginning is U.10 After an output (in, x) the input
alphabet is Y, and after an output of the form (out, v) the input alphabet is U.
There is a finite upper bound on the number of consecutive outputs of the form
(in, x).
Definition 3.9. The application of a ((U, V), (X , Y))-DDC α to a (X , Y)-DDS s
yields the (U, V)-DDS, denoted as αs, described (informally) above. Upon an
input u ∈ U to αs, input u is given to α. Whenever α outputs (in, x), then x is
given as input to s⊥ , and if y ∈ Y ∪ { ⊥} is the output of s⊥ , then y is given as
input to α. Whenever α outputs (out, v), then v is defined to be the output of αs
for the most recent input u ∈ U to αs).
We do not give a completely formal definition of the appliction of a converter
to a system. Formally, one would have to show that the described object αs is
indeed a (U, V)-DDS. Intuitively, this is obvious.

3.4.3 Filters
A special type of converter, which we call a filter, is one that restricts the domain
dom(s) of a DDS s to a subset of dom(s) (again closed under taking prefixes). If
we denote the filter by ϕ, then ϕs is a DDS with domain
dom(ϕs) ⊆ dom(s)
and such that
(ϕs)(x1 , . . . , xk ) = s(x1 , . . . , xk )
for all (x1 , . . . , xk ) ∈ dom(ϕs).
The simplest type of filter is one that restricts access to s to a certain fixed
number q of queries. Thus [q]s is the system s restricted to q queries and is
undefined as of the (q+1)-st query.
Definition 3.10. The filter that restricts access to q queries is denoted as [q].

3.4.4 Cascading Systems
In this and the next subsection we discuss two natural ways for combining discrete systems.
10 This means that the function is undefined for inputs outside of U .

62

Definition 3.11. Consider an (X , Y)-DDS s and a (Y, Z)-DDS t. The cascade of s
and t, denoted s ⊲ t, is the (X , Z)-DDS defined as follows. The input x ∈ X (to
s ⊲ t) is given as input to s, the output y ∈ Y of s (if defined) is given as input to
t, and the output z ∈ Z of t (if defined) is returned as the output of s ⊲ t:
(s ⊲ t)(x1 , . . . , xk ) = t(y1 , . . . , yk ),
where, for 1 ≤ j ≤ k,

yj = s(x1 , . . . , xj ).

If either value is undefined, the output of s ⊲ t is undefined.
One can alternatively define a converter casc which implements s ⊲ t when
given parallel access to s and t, i.e., such that
casc[s, t] = s ⊲ t.

3.4.5 Combining the Outputs of Systems
Definition 3.12. Consider two (X , Y)-DDS s and t. For an operation11 ⋆ on Y
the system s ⋆ t is the (X , Y)-DDS where the input is given to both s and t and
their outputs are combined using ⋆ to obtain the output of s ⋆ t:
(s ⋆ t)(x1 , . . . , xk ) = s(x1 , . . . , xk ) ⋆ t(x1 , . . . , xk ).
If either value is undefined, the output of s ⋆ t is undefined.
One can alternatively define a converter comb∗ which implements s ⋆ t when
given parallel access to s and t, i.e., such that
comb⋆ [s, t] = s ⋆ t.

3.4.6 Connecting a Converter to an Interface
Recall Definition 3.5 of a DDS with interface set I. We define the operation of
applying a converter α at interface i of such a (P × X , Y)-DDS s.

Definition 3.13. The application of a ((X , Y), (X , Y))-DDC α at interface i of a
(P × X , Y)-DDS s, denoted as αi s, is the (P × X , Y)-DDS defined as follows.
Inputs at interfaces different from i are handled as before. An input (i, x) (at
interface i) is handled as follows: x is given as input to α, and all outputs of α of
the form (in, x) are given to s⊥ as (i, x). Once α outputs (out, y), y is the output
of the DDS αi s for the input (i, x).
We state the following lemma without a proof.

Lemma 3.1. Appending converters at distinct interfaces commutes: For i 6= j we have
αi β j s = β j αi s.

63

3.5 Probabilistic Systems
We now define systems with a probabilistic behavior. To make things easy, we
stay in the realm of discrete probability theory (avoiding σ-algebras and measure theory) and consider only finite sample spaces. In many cases one can
consider a random experiment with an infinite sample space as corresponding
to an infinite sequence of random experiments with finite sample spaces.
Definition 3.14. A probabilistic discrete (X , Y)-system (an (X , Y)-PDS for short) is
a random variable over a set of (X , Y)-DDS.
A PDS, which we typically denote by a capital bold-face symbol such as S,
is characterized by the probability distribution (over DDS).
A probabilistic system can, like deterministic systems, be described in many
different ways, for example by appropriately modifying the description of a
deterministic system, say by introducing a randomness space R and making
everything depend on the random value r chosen from R. Many different descriptions can correspond to the same PDS. We do not focus on descriptions of
systems, but on the mathematical object described by a particular description.
Definition 3.15. An (X , Y)-random function (for finite X and Y) is a random
variable taking on functions X → Y as values. It corresponds to the system
which for each input replies with the function value. In particular, equal inputs
are always answered by identical outputs.
An X -random permutation is the special case where Y = X and only bijective
functions X → X have non-zero probability.
Example 3.5. A uniform
random function [permutation] is called URF [URP].

A {0, 1}m, {0, 1}n -URF is often denoted as Rm,n and a {0, 1}m-URP is often
denoted as Pm . When m and n are understood, the subscripts are also dropped.

Definition 3.16. A probabilistic discrete environment for an (X , Y)-DDS (a (Y, X )PDE for short) is a random variable over a set of (Y, X )-DDE.

The transcript t r(S, E) of a probabilistic system S in a probabilistic environment E is well-defined via the function t r(·, ·) (see Definition 3.7). In the random
experiment consisting of selecting, independently, a system s and an environment e according to their respective distributions, t r(S, E) is the random variable taking on as values sequences x1 , y1 , x2 , y2 , x3 , y3 , . . . and can essentially
equivalently be understood as the sequence t r(S, E) = X1 , Y1 , X2 , Y2 , X3 , Y3 , . . .
of random variables.
Definition 3.17. A probabilistic discrete converter (a PDC for short) (for given alphabets) is a random variable over a set of DDC (for the same alphabets).
The composition of a probabilistic converter with a probabilistic system is a
probabilistic system and is defined naturally via the function (α, s) 7→ αs, where
the arguments and hence also the function value are random variables.
64

3.6 The Behavior of Discrete Systems
A (probabilistic) system S defines the probability distribution of the transcript
t r(S, E) for every environment E. To characterize the complete observable behavior of a system, it suffices to define the transcript distribution for every possible environment E. Surprisingly, two different PDS S and T (i.e., two different
random variables over (X , Y)-DDS) can behave identically in any environment
and can hence be considered equivalent (see Definition 3.19 below).12 We hence
want to define the mathematical object of the behavior os a probabilistic system.

3.6.1 A First Step: Channels in Communication Theory
To address the problem of defining the mathematical type of the input-output
behavior of a discrete system, we first consider a simple type of system C, which
only takes a single input from alphabet X and produces and output from alphabet Y. In information theory and communication theory such a system is often
called a discrete memoryless channel and is defined by a conditional probability
distribution13
pCY |X ,
where the superscript C makes explicit which system we consider. Such a channel is characterized exactly by specifying, for each x ∈ X and y ∈ Y, the probability that output y is generated when input x is given, which we can denote
as pCY |X (y, x). We point out that defining pCY |X does not define a random experiment. This is the reason why we use a small p to avoid suggesting that a random
experiment (and hence probabilities) are defined (at this point).
In contrast, if we follow our approach taken so far, we can define a deterministic channel c is a function c : X → Y, a DDS with a single input x and
corresponding output y. A probabilistic channel C is thus a random variable
over functions X → Y.
Such a probabilistic channel defines the conditional distribution pC
Y |X (y, x),

3.6.3 Equivalence of Probabilistic Systems
Different probabilistic systems can have the same behavior, which motivates the
following equivalence relation.
Definition 3.19. Two probabilistic (X , Y)-systems S and T are equivalent, denoted S ≡ T, if they have the same behavior, i.e., if b(S) = b(T).

The probability on the right side is in the random experiment of choosing S at
random, which is made explicit by the superscript S. Similarly, to determine
pS

3.7 Discrete Computational Problems
3.7.1 Discrete Games
A (deterministic) discrete game G can be described by a discrete (X , Y)-system
which interacts with its environment by taking inputs x1 , x2 , . . . and answering
18 To be entirely explicit, we have put the random variables defining the random experiment (i.e.,
ES) as a superscript also for (conditional) probability distributions of random variables.

70

with outputs y1 , y2 , . . .. In addition, after every input it also generates a bit ai
indicating whether the game has been won. This bit is monotone in the sense
that it is initially set to 0 and that, once it has turned to 1 (i.e., the game is won),
it does not turn back to 0 (even if the game were continued). This motivates the
following definition, which captures the notion of game-winning.
Definition 3.22. For a (X , Y × {0, 1})-DDS g the binary component ai of the
output (yi , ai ) is called a monotone binary output (MBO) if ai = 1 implies aj = 1
for j ≥ i. Such a DDS g with MBO is also called a deterministic discrete (X , Y)game (DDG).
Obviously, a probabilistic discrete game G is now a probabilistic discrete
system (PDS, see Definition 3.14) with MBO.
A (deterministic) winner w for an (X , Y)-game g generates x1 as an input to
g, receives the output y1 of g as its (first) input, then generates x2 , receives y2 ,
etc.
Definition 3.23. A (deterministic) winner w for an (X , Y)-game g is a discrete
(Y, X )-environment (DDE, see Definition 3.16).
Note that the MBO ai is not returned to w. One can think of ai as being
output at a special interface of g which we can think of as depicted at the right
side of g. We can nevertheless understand ai to be part of the transcript. The
transcript (see Definition 3.7) is hence
t r(g, w) = x1 , (y1 , a1 ), x2 , (y2 , a2 ), x3 , (y3 , a3 ), . . .
Winner w wins game g if ai = 1 for some i ≥ 1.
Whether the winner “sees” ai or not may appear to be irrelevant since all
we care about is whether the game is won. However, for so-called multi-games,
the natural generalization where the game has several MBO’s, it does matter
whether these bits are (or are not) hidden from the winner. We point out that
even though the MBO’s are hidden, a DDG can of course be defined in a way
that the regular outputs yi contain information about ai or even explicitly contains a copy of ai .

3.7.2 Discrete Distinguishers
Definition 3.24. A (deterministic) discrete distinguisher for (X , Y)-system (DDD)
is an environment d for (X , Y)-systems which, when it stops, also outputs a
bit. More formally, it is an environment with two stop symbols ⊣0 and ⊣1 . The
output of d for system s is equal to the index of the stopping symbol. If d does
not stop (i.e., never outputs ⊣0 or ⊣1 ), then the output is defined to be 0.

Chapter 4

Computational Problems and
Reductions
4.1 Preliminaries
Computational problems and reductions between problems have been studied
and formalized in complexity theory. The types of problems arising in cryptography are more general, in several ways, than those usually considered in
complexity theory.

4.1.1 Decision Problems
In classical complexity theory (where classes such as P and NP are defined),
an important type of computational problem are decision problems. A decision
problem is characterized by an instance set X and a predicate P : X → {0, 1}.
The problem consists of deciding, for a given x ∈ X , whether P (x) = 1.
A specific type of decision problem is characterized by a predicate Q : X ×
W → {0, 1}, where X is an instance set and W is a witness set. The problem
consists of deciding, for a given x ∈ X , whether there exists a witness w ∈ W
such that Q(x, w) = 1, i.e., P (x) := ∃w Q(x, w).
Example 4.1. Decide whether a given graph with n vertices contains a Hamiltonian cycle. The instance set is (some subset of) the set of Boolean n × n matrices
(the adjacency matrix of a graph), the witness set is the set of (somehow encoded) paths of length n, and the predicate Q consisting of verifying that a path
is a cycle in the graph. This problem is known to be NP-complete.1
1 see M.R. Garey and D.S. Johnson, Computers and Intractability – A Guide to the Theory of NPCompleteness, New York: W.H. Freeman and Co., 1979.

71

72

Example 4.2. Decide whether a given number is a prime number. This problem
has been shown to be in P.2

4.1.2 Search Problems
The search problem corresponding to a decision problem (of the above type) is
the problem of finding a witness w (which can be called a solution) for a given x.
Definition 4.1. A search problem is a 4-tuple (X , W, Q, PX ) consisting of an instance set X , a solution set (or witness set) W, a predicate Q : X × W → {0, 1}, as
well as a probability distribution PX over the instance set.3
The search problem (X , W, Q, PX ) consists of finding, for a given instance
x ∈ X drawn according to PX , a w ∈ W such that Q(x, w) = 1.4 Typically one
considers cases where every instance x has at least one solution w, but other
cases can also be considered by allowing a special output “no solution”.
Example 4.3. Finding a Hamiltonian cycle in a graph with n vertices (see Example 4.1). A random graph with n nodes containing a Hamiltonian cycle can,
for example, be generated by starting from an n-cycle and then adding random
edges according to some distribution.
Exercise 4.1. Suppose that for a search problem there exists a probabilistic algorithm with average success probability 1%. Can one amplify the success probability by repeating the algorithm several times?
Example 4.4. The discrete logarithm (DL) problem (see Definition 2.12) for a
group G = hgi is a search problem where the instance set is G, the solution set
is Zq , and the predicate Q is defined as
Q(x, w) = 1

⇐⇒

g w = x,

and the instance distribution PX is the uniform distribution.
Example 4.5. Function inversion. The problem of inverting a function f :
{0, 1}n → {0, 1}m will be denoted as I f (I stands for inversion.). The instance
set is X = {0, 1}m, the solution set is W = {0, 1}n , and the predicate Q is defined
as
Q(x, w) = 1 ⇐⇒ f (w) = x.

The distribution PX is usually assumed to be the distribution implied by the
uniform distribution on the input of f , i.e., X = f (U ), where U is a uniform
n-bit string. Note that the DL problem is also a function inversion problem. A
function f for which the function inversion problem is computationally hard is
called a one-way function.

4.1.3 Beyond Worst-case Analyses
In traditional complexity theory, one usually considers only algorithms that
work for all instances, i.e., in the worst case. Such problems do not specify
an instance distribution, i.e., they can be considered to be triples (X , W, Q). In
cryptography, the worst-case hardness of a problem is useless. Even if (the computational problem of) breaking a cryptosystem is hard in the worst case (for example NP-complete), it is still possible that the problem is easy with substantial
probability or even with very high probability. What is required in cryptography are statements about almost-everywhere hardness. This forces us to reconsider the notion of success of an algorithm for solving a certain computational
problem and the notion of a reduction.

4.1.4 Problems Relevant in Cryptography
We briefly repeat what was already discussed in Section 2.3: The three types of
computationl problems relevant in cryptography.
In cryptography, an important class of computational problems are so-called
games. In a game, the instance is not only a value, as in a search problem, but
an instance is an interactive process or discrete system with which an algorithm
(trying to win the game) can interact in several steps. The game is characterized by a special winning condition. The algorithm succeeds if it provokes this
winning condition to become true. Search problems are a special type of game
which are non-interactive.
Example 4.6. The security definition of a MAC (see Definition 2.11) can be
phrased as a game which the adversary is supposed to win. The game is interactive.
Another important class of computational problems in cryptography are socalled distinction problems. The task of an algorithm is to determine which of two
options (e.g. values) it is given access to.
Example 4.7. The so-called Decisional Diffie-Hellman (DDH) problem (see Definition 2.14) is a distinction problem.
Similarly to the generalization from search problems to interactive games,
distinction problems can be generalized to a setting where for each option not
only a value, but access to an interactive system is provided.
Example 4.8. The real-world system and the ideal-world system for symmetric
encryption (see Section 2.4.4) are simple examples of interactive systems, where
the distinguishing algorithm can input a message at interface A and observe the
outputs at interfaces B and E.

2 see M. Agrawal, N. Kayal, and N. Saxena, PRIMES is in P, Annals of Mathematics, 160 (2): 781–
793, 2004.
3 Often, but not always, X ⊆ {0, 1}∗ , W ⊆ {0, 1}∗ , and P is the uniform distribution.
X
4 One can distinguish two types of search problems, depending on whether or not Q is efficiently
computable. If Q is efficiently computable, then problems with a small solution set W are easy to
solve by an exhaustive search.

Closely related to distinction problem are bit-guessing problem where the task
is, when given access to a system, to guess a bit hidden within the system. The
two types of problems can be shown to be in a certain sense equivalent.

74

4.2 Example 1: Security of the RSA LSB
Recall the reduction concept mentioned in Section 1.5.2. We introduce a first example of a non-trivial cryptographically interesting reduction. We describe it in
a conventional language (using terms like efficient algorithm); our terminology
and concepts will be introduced later.
The security of the RSA trapdoor one-way permutation relies on the computational hardness of computing e-th roots modulo n. However, even if this
problem is hard, it is conceivable that finding certain parts of the e-th root (i.e.,
of the message in the naı̈ve RSA system), for example the least significant bit
(LSB) is nevertheless easy. In this section we prove the surprising result that
finding the least significant bit of an RSA-plaintext from a given ciphertext is
computationally equivalent to finding the entire plaintext.
Theorem 4.1. If there exists an efficient algorithm A which for a given k-bit RSAmodulus n, exponent e, and ciphertext c, computes the LSB of the corresponding plaintext m (where c = me ), then there exists an efficient algorithm which for a given c
computes the entire corresponding m (by calling algorithm A k times as a subroutine).
e

i.e, A(c) = LSB(m). Such an algorithm A can be exploited as follows.
By the multiplicative property of the RSA function we (i.e., the reduction
algorithm) can easily compute
(2m)e = 2e me = 2e c,

modulo n (i.e., n is subtracted). Because n is odd we have
A(2e c) = 0 ⇐⇒ m < n/2.
By a similar argument one can show that

Let us discuss the significance (or insignificance) of Theorem 4.1. It seems to
state that a single-bit message b can be securely encrypted by choosing a random
m with LSB b. (Note that this is a randomized PKE scheme.) However, Theorem 4.1 is not strong enough to prove the IND-CPA security of this scheme.
One would need that guessing the LSB correctly with probability significantly
greater than 0.5 allows to invert the RSA function.5 Indeed, such a stronger
statement can be proved. Moreover, an even stronger result can be proved:6
The log k least significant bits are simultaneously unapproximable if inverting
the RSA function is hard. More precisely, an algorithm which distinguishes
the log k least significant bits of a random plaintext from uniformly random bits
with non-negligibly advantage can be used to compute the entire plaintext. This
shows that one could use the naı̈ve RSA encryption (with randomization) to encrypt a few bits.
We note that a reduction statement of this type can be read in two different
directions, which are each valid:
• The least significant bits are secure if we (only) assume the hardness of
inverting the RSA function.


Each value A( 2i )e c allows us to exclude one half of the remaining possible
region for m. For instance, if A(2e c) = 1 we know that m > n/2. If in addition
A(4e c) = 0 we know that n/2 < m < 3n/4. Similarly, A(8e c) = 1 tells us
that 5n/8 < m < 3n/4, etc. After k applications of algorithm A one ends up
with a single value m. In other words, we can determine the binary expansion
m
e
of m/n, bit-by-bit: A(2e c) is the LSB of ⌊2 m
n ⌋, A(4 c) is the LSB of ⌊4 n ⌋, etc.
Hence, we have proved the following lemma, which concludes the proof of the
theorem.

as hard as the whole, SIAM Journal on Computing, 17(2):194–209, 1988.

• The RSA function can be inverted if one can slightly distinguish the least
significant bits from random. This task appears much easier than inverting
RSA, and this can be seen as casting doubt on the security of RSA.
We also note that in a reasonable cryptographic design, the RSA-function is

usually only used as a TOWP and not as a (naı̈ve) public-key encryption scheme.
In this case the relevance of understanding the security of the LSB is unclear.

4.3 Example 2: Guessing the LSB of a Discrete Logarithm
4.3.1 Introduction
In this section we discuss another example of a cryptographic reduction. This
example is more interesting than that of the previous section, for several reasons. First, it is related to the result of a famous paper.7 Second, the reduction
goes all the way from the hardness of computing a value (a discrete logarithm)
to showing that a certain bit cannot even be guessed with a slight advantage
over a random guess. This result can be used to obtain a pseudo-random generator based on the sole assumption that the DL problem in the given group is
worst-case hard. Third, the reduction is obtained in a sequence of steps, which
can each be understood as an individual reduction, where the overall statement
is obtained as the composition of these individual reductions.
The overall treatment is quite involved, and this provides motivation for our
abstract approach to defining and understanding reductions, which is to follow
in the rest of the chapter.
Already in this section we use terminology introduced later in the chapter:
We use the term solver (instead of algorithm) for the object that solves a computational problem. We also use the term performance to measure the amount of
success of a solver:8
• For a game, the performance of a solver is the probability of winning the
game (see Definition 2.5).
• For a bit-guessing problem like the LSB-problem, the performance of a
solver is the advantage in guessing the bit correctly, i.e., 2p − 1 if p is the
probability of guessing the bit correctly (see Definition 2.9).
A worst-case problem means that one requires a solver to achieve a certain
performance for all instances of the problem. In other words, the performance of
a solver for a worst-case problem is defined as the minimum of the performance
over all instances of the problem.
An average-case problem means that one requires a solver to achieve a certain
performance on average over the instances of the problem chosen according to
a specified distribution.

4.3.2 The Problem Statement
We consider a group H = hhi of order |H| = m, generated by the generator h,
and we consider two computational problems for H:
• the DL-problem in H, i.e., given y = hx , compute x;

• the LSB-problem in H, i.e., given y = hx , compute the least significant bit
(LSB) of x.

Since the LSB-problem is easy for even group order (see exercise below) we
assume from now on that |H| = m is odd.
Exercise 4.2. Show that if the group order m is even, then guessing the LSB is
easy even in the worst case, i.e., there exists an efficient solver for the worst-case
LSB-problem with performance 1.
We use the following notation to denote different computational problems
for the group H. We use DL and LSB to refer to discrete logarithm and LSB problems, respectively. A set I as superscript for I ⊆ H denotes the worst-case
problem for the instance set I. Often I = H. A H-valued random variable Y as
superscript denotes the average-case problem when the instance is (chosen according to the distribution of) Y . A uniform random variable over H is denoted
as H. The computational problems that will arise in our context are:
• DLH denotes the worst-case DL-problem (for the full instance set H).

• DLI denotes the worst-case DL-problem for an instance set I ⊆ H.
• DLH denotes the average-case DL-problem (uniform over H).

• LSBH denotes the worst-case LSB-problem (for the full instance set H).

• LSBI denotes the worst-case LSB-problem for an instance set I ⊆ H.
• LSBH denotes the average-case LSB-problem (uniform over H).

Our goal is to prove that if the DL-problem is hard (the assumption) then
the apparently much simpler problem of guessing (only) the least significant
bit (LSB) of the discrete logarithm, is also hard (the conclusion). The goal is to
prove such a statement
• for the weakest possible form of the assumption, and

• for the strongest possible form of the conclusion.

7 see Manuel Blum and Silvio Micali, How to Generate Cryptographically Strong Sequences of
Pseudorandom Bits, SIAM Journal on Computing. vol. 13, no. 4, 1984, pp. 850–864.
8 The term performance does not refer to efficiency or speed.

The (weak) assumption is that solving the worst-case DL-problem DLH with
performance close to 1 is hard. This means that there is no efficient algorithm
that can solve every instance of the DL-problem with probability at least 1−δ, for
some small δ > 0. Note that this assumption is weaker than the assumption that
solving the worst-case DL-problem with performance ǫ for some ǫ > 0 is hard,

77

78

and it is also weaker then the assumption that the average-case DL-problem
DLH with performance 1 − δ is hard. In other words, our assumption does not
exclude the existence of an efficient worst-case DL-algorithm that works only
mildly well or one that works very well on average.
The (strong) conclusion is that the average-case LSB-guessing problem LSBH
is hard, even if only a small advantage (i.e., a guessing probability slightly above
0.5) is required. Note that this is a (much) stronger statement than that the
worst-case LSB-problem LSBH is hard.
The described statement corresponds to the following theorem, which is a
reduction statement. It is only informally stated; what “simple operations” is
not made formal.
Theorem 4.3. For any δ, ǫ > 0, and for any solver S for LSBH with performance at
least ǫ, there exists a solver for DLH with performance at least 1 − δ which invokes S a
polynomial (in log(1/δ) and 1/ǫ and log m) number of times and otherwise performs
only a few simple operations.9
From this theorem one can conclude the following corollary, assuming that
negligible is made precise in an adequate manner. This is a typical formulation
of Theorem 4.3 appearing in the literature.
Corollary 4.4. If for some δ < 1 there exists no polynomial-time algorithm for solving
DLH with performance at least 1 − δ, then there exists no polynomial-time algorithm for
solving LSBH with non-negligible performance.
Theorem 4.3 is proved by the composition of a sequence of reductions. We
develop a natural line of thinking in which these individual reductions are developed, making explicit the various interesting and clever ideas that one needs
in order to succeed.

4.3.3 Idea 1: Using an LSB-oracle to Compute the DL
Recall that raising any element y ∈ H to the group order m yields the neutral
element (denoted as 1), i.e., we have
y m = 1.
We first describe two useful operations on group elements. Given y = hx with
x > 0 one can reduce x by 1 (modulo m), i.e., one can compute y ′ = hx−1 ) as
follows:
y ′ = y · h−1 .

Moreover, the (unique) square root z of y = hx can be computed as
z = y (m+1)/2
because z 2 = y m+1 = y. If x is even, then z = hx/2 , i.e., the binary representation
of x is shifted to the right by one bit.
If we have a solver S for LSBH that is always correct (i.e., with performance 1),
then a solver T for DLH can be obtained as follows. Given an instance y = hx ,
T determines the LSB of x by invoking S. Then, T computes y ′ for which the
LSB of x is masked: If the LSB of x is 1 (i.e., x is odd), T computes y ′ = y · h−1 ,
′
otherwise it sets y ′ = y. If we define x′ by y ′ = hx , then x′ is even. Thus T can
shift (the binary representation of) x′ to the right by one bit by computing
′

z = y ′(m+1)/2 = hx /2 = h⌊x/2⌋ .
Then T sets y = z and repeats the above steps until z = h, thus computing all
the bits of x, and hence x. This requires at most ⌈log2 x⌉ ≤ ⌈log2 m⌉ invocations
of S.
We next observe that the above argument also works if S guesses the LSB
correctly only with probability at least 1 − δ ′ /2 for any instance, i.e., if it has
performance 1 − δ ′ for LSBH . In this case, the constructed solver T for DLH has
success probability (and hence performance) at least 1 − δ ′ ⌈log2 x⌉/2. This follows from the union bound of probability theory.10 The algorithm is incorrect
only if at least one of the invocations of S yields a wrong result. Since we will
want the performance to be at least 1 − δ, one has to choose δ ′ accordingly.

4.3.4 Idea 2: From Worst-Case to Average Case
We actually have to work with a much weaker LSB-solver, namely one for which
only the average-case performance is guaranteed and, moreover, where the performance is very small, namely only guaranteed to be at least ǫ.
A technique that sometimes works for solving the first problem is to randomize the instance so that it becomes an average-case instance. A technique
that sometimes works for solving the second problem is to amplify the performance by repetition (see idea 4 below).
A given instance y = g x can be randomized by choosing a random r ∈ Zm
and computing the randomized instance
y ′ = y · g r = g x+r
(where exponents are reduced modulo m). Unfortunately, this randomization
does not work because if
x + r ≥ m,

9 The theorem is not a totally precise mathemtical statement since we have not defined “a few
simple operations”, but the proof will make explicit which operations are needed and one will see
that they are simple. One could make a more formal statement involving the notion of polynomialtime.

10 The union bounds states the simple fact that the probability of the union of events is upper
bounded by the sum of the probabilities.

79

80

i.e., if x + r is reduced modulo m (which we call an overflow). In this case, the
LSB changes because m is odd. Since x is unknown, one does not know whether
the LSB was changed, and hence the LSB-solver’s answer for the instance y ′ is
useless.
We therefore need another idea and come back to idea 2 in Section 4.3.7.

4.3.5 Idea 3: Restriction to the Initial Segment
The above randomization technique would work if x were known to be very
small. So the following idea can be used. For a fixed integer parameter n, we
divide the interval [0, m − 1] for x into n segments of length ⌈m/n⌉ (where the
last segment can be shorter) and assume we have a solver that works if x is in
the first segment. Let
I = {h0 , h1 , . . . , h⌈m/n⌉−1 } ⊆ H
denote the “initial segment” of the instance set of the problems we consider.
The worst-case DL- and LSB-problems for instances in I are denoted as DLI and
LSBI , respectively. Moreover, let
t = ⌈log2 (⌊m/n⌋ + 1)⌉
be the number of bits needed to represent the discrete logarithm of an element
of I.
For this idea to work we need to show three things.
• First, a solver S for DLI can be used to construct a solver for DLH (this is
idea 3). If we have a worst-case solver S for the initial segment, i.e., for
DLI , then we can obtain a worst-case solver T for DLH , as follows. For
ℓ = 0, . . . , n − 1, T computes
y ′ = y · g −ℓ⌈m/n⌉ = g x−ℓ⌈m/n⌉

and invokes S for instance y ′ . One of these shifted instances is guaranteed
to be in I. If a solution x′ is reported by S, then it is tested for correctness
(which is possible efficiently), and the shift is adjusted to obtain the correct
DL-value:
x = x′ + ℓ⌈m/n⌉.
T needs to invoke S at most n times.
I

4.3.6 Idea 4: Performance Amplification for Worst-Case Solvers
Given a solver S for LSBI with performance ǫ′ we can construct a solver T for
LSBI with performance 1 − δ ′ by statistical amplification: For an adequate parameter q, T invokes S q times and then takes a majority decision. By the law
of large numbers, it suffices to choose q roughly on the order of 1/(ǫ′ )2 and
log(1/δ ′ ).
Exercise 4.3. Work this out.

4.3.7 Making Idea 2 Work: Randomizing the Instance
A solver S for LSBH (i.e., for the uniform distribution) with performance ǫ can be
used to construct a solver T for LSBI with performance ǫ′ , for ǫ′ to be discussed
below, as follows.
Given y = g x with y ∈ I, T chooses an offset r uniformly at random from
[0, k − 1] for k discussed below, adds the offset r to x by computing
y ′ = y · hr = hx+r ,
calls S (once) for the instance y ′ , receives the answer b of S, and returns b ⊕ r0 ,
where r0 is the LSB of r.11
To avoid an overflow (i.e., x + r ≥ m), we must have 0 ≤ r ≤ k − 1 for
k = m − ⌊m/n⌋.
Note that x + k − 1 < m. An instance x ∈ I is hence randomized to a uniform
instance in the interval
J(x) = {hx , hx+1 , . . . , hx+k−1 },
which depends on x. The size of this interval satisfies
|J(x)| = k = m − ⌊m/n⌋ ≥ m(1 − 1/n).

4.3.8 Idea 5: Changing the Instance Distribution

• Second, we need to observe that for instances of DL , the above-described
technique for using an LSBH -solver to construct a DLH -solver (idea 1 above)
works also if we only have an LSBI -solver. This is the case because all
instances for which the LSB-solver S is invoked have exponents in the
interval I.

The problem with this approach is that we consider the computational problem
LSBYx for Yx uniform in J(x) instead of the problem LSBH (for H uniform in H),
for which we assumed that the performance of S is at least ǫ. This problem
can be addressed by using the following fact (which the reader can prove as an
exercise).

• Third, we need to investigate how the randomization and amplification
technique works for instances known to be in I (see below).

11 The answer of S must be complemented if r is odd (by computing b ⊕ r ) because if b is the
0
correct LSB of x + r, then b ⊕ r0 is the correct LSB of x (provided there is no overflow).
