Exercise 4.4. Prove that for a bit-guessing problem and a solver S for it, if one
changes the instance distribution by at most d in terms of statistical distance,
then the performance of S changes by at most 2d.12 Also state this formally.
Exercise 4.5. Prove that in our example, the statistical distance between the instance distribution of LSBYx (for any x) and LSBH is at most 1/n, i.e., for all x ∈ I,
δ(Yx , H) ≤ 1/n.
Using the results of these two exercises, we conclude that the performance
of solver T for LSBYx is at least
ǫ′ = ǫ − 2/n.

• DLI is reduced to LSBI (idea 1): The solver for LSBI is called t =
⌈log2 (⌊m/n⌋ + 1)⌉ times. If the performance for LSBI is 1 − δ ′ , then the
performance for DLI is 1 − 2tδ ′ . Since this should be at least 1 − δ, we have
to choose δ ′ = δ/(2t).
• LSBI is reduced to itself (idea 4): The solver for LSBI is called q times, such
that the performance is amplified from ǫ′ to 1 − δ ′ .

• LSBI is reduced to LSBYx (idea 2): The solver for LSBYx is called once, and
the performance remains identical (namely ǫ′ ).
• LSBYx is reduced to LSBH (idea 5): The solver is the same but the performance is reduced by 2/n from ǫ to ǫ′ = ǫ − 2/n.

4.3.11 Some Remarks
4.3.9 Putting Things Together
For given δ and ǫ in Theorem 4.3, one can choose the parameters n and q involved in the above reductions so as to obtain an overall reduction with as small
as possible complexity blow-up.
The parameter n must be chosen so that ǫ > 2/n since otherwise ǫ′ is not
positive. One can for example choose n = 4/ǫ. The parameter q must be chosen
sufficiently large so as to provide the necessary amplification.

We point out that, formally, the reduction of LSBI to LSBYx via randomization is
actually a reduction for a specific x, i.e., for a problem with a single instance. The
claimed reduction holds for every x, and the reduction of LSBYx to LSBH (idea 5)
also works for every x ∈ I. Therefore their combination works for every x ∈ I
and hence for the problem LSBI .13

4.4 Abstract Computational Problems and Reductions

4.3.10 Summary
Let us summarize the complete argument, which consists of five steps, each
making use of one of the ideas and providing a reduction of a computational
problem Q to another computational problem P (where P = Q is possible).
Each such reduction is characterized by the following two factors:
• The mapping of a solver S for P to a solver T for Q.

• The mapping translating the performance of S to the performance of T .

The five relevant computational problems are DLH , DLI , LSBI , LSBYx , and LSBH .
The five reduction steps, in the order as they are composed (not in the order in
which they were discussed), are:
• DLH is reduced to DLI (idea 3): The given instance is shifted (at most) n
times such that one of the shifted instances lies in the initial segment I.
The solver for DLI must be called at most n times, and the performance
remains the same.
12 The same statement holds for games, without the factor 2.

83

4.4.1 Introduction
The goal of complexity theory is to investigate the computational complexity
of computational problems. More precisely, one wants to characterize the best
achievable performance in solving the problem as a function of the complexity
of the solver. In other words, for a given complexity value c one maximizes the
achievable performance over all solvers with complexity at most c.
To achieve this goal, one would need a concrete computational model in
which complexity is defined. The choice of such a model would, at least to
some extent, be arbitrary, and such a treatment would detract from the essence
of what reductions are about. We therefore provide a more abstract treatment
of reductions. However, one could always take a more concrete viewpoint by
thinking of a solver as the set of all algorithms of a given complexity, i.e., one
can think of a solver as being a complexity value if one insists on taking a
complexity-based viewpoint.
13 This illustrates (once more) that reductions make sense even for problems which are not hard,
for example a problem with a single instance. Reductions are not only used to prove hardness
implications.

84

4.4.2 Preliminaries

4.4.4 Upper Bounds on Performance

For the composition of functions f and g we write gf instead of g ◦ f . Hence we
write, for example, hgf (a) instead of h(g(f (a))). Sometimes we write simply
f a instead of f (a).
For functions fi : Ai → Bi (for 1 ≤ i ≤ k), (f1 , . . . , fk ) denotes the direct
product of f1 , . . . , fk , i.e., the function A1 × · · · × Ak → B1 × · · · × Bk defined by

An important goal in cryptography and complexity theory is to prove that a
certain problem p is hard (e.g., the problem of breaking a cryptosystem). Ideally,
one wants to prove an upper bound on the performance of any solver for p, i.e.,
a statement of the form ∀s p (s) ≤ ǫ for some small ǫ. This can also be written
simply as
p ≤ ǫ,

(f1 , . . . , fk )(a1 , . . . , ak ) = (f1 (a1 ), . . . , fk (ak )).
For functions fi : A → Bi (for 1 ≤ i ≤ k), [f1 , . . . , fk ] denotes the function
A → B1 × · · · × Bk defined by

[f1 , . . . , fk ](a) = f1 (a), . . . , fk (a) .

The function assigning to a list of real numbers their sum is denoted as sum.
For example, sum(3, 4, 5) = 12. Similarly, for boolean-valued lists we denote by
∧ (or ∨) the logical AND (or logical OR) of the values. For functions f : A → B
and g : A → B, where
a partial order ≤ is defined on B, we write f ≤ g to mean

∀a f (a) ≤ g(a) .

4.4.3 Problems, Solvers, and Performance
In a theory of computation, at the most abstract level one considers two types
of objects.
• Problems are objects that should be computed or solved.
• Solvers are objects that compute or solve a problem.
Definition 4.2. A problem p is (an object) equipped with a set Σp of solvers, a
partially ordered set (Ωp ; ≤) of performance values14 , and a performance function
p : Σp → Ωp
assigning a performance value p (s) to every solver s ∈ Σp . A solver s for which
p (s) ≥ a for a ∈ Ωp is called an a-solver for p.
Performance values are often real numbers (i.e., Ωp ⊆ R), for example a
success probability or a distinguishing advantage, but one can also consider
more general performance sets, for example pairs of real numbers. We will
consider different types of problems, for example games with performance set
[0, 1] and distinction problems with performance set [−1, 1].
14 Here a ≤ b means that the performance value b is (in some sense) at least as good as a.

85

where ǫ is understood as the constant function Σp → Ωp mapping every solver
to the value ǫ. (Note that usually ǫ is real-valued.) Such a statement is often
called information-theoretic or unconditional since it holds for any solver. In particular, it does not depend on the implementation complexity.
However, since for a usual computational problem p there always exists a
(possibly inefficient) solver that solves the problem well (i.e., with high performance), one proves a weaker statement where the upper bound depends on
the performance function q of another problem q. Such a statement is called a
reduction. More generally, p can also be upper bounded in terms of the performance functions qi (i = 1, . . . , k) of several problems.

4.4.5 The Reduction Concept
An important concept in computer science is to use a solver s for a problem p
with performance at least a (i.e., p (s) ≥ a) to construct a solver s′ for a problem
q with performance at least a′ (i.e., q (s′ ) ≥ a′ ), where s′ = ρ (s) for a function
ρ : Σp → Σq

(called the reduction function) and where a′ = τ (a) for a ≤-respecting function
τ : Ωp → Ωq

(called the performance translation function).15 This can be summarized by the
function inequality
τ p ≤ qρ
(4.1)
which states that for any a-solver s for p, ρ (s) is a (τ (a))-solver for q:
∀s ∈ Σp : τ p (s) ≤ q ρ (s).
In the following, performance translation functions will tacitly be assumed to
be ≤-respecting.
A special case of (4.1) is p ≤ q ρ , where τ is the identity function, i.e., ρ
transforms a solver for q into an equally good solver for p. Another special case
of (4.1) is τ p ≤ q , where ρ is the identity function, i.e., every solver for p is also
a solver for q with performance related via the function τ .
15 τ

is ≤-respecting if

a ≤ b ⇒ τ (a) ≤ τ (b),
i.e., a better solver for s does not result in a worse solver for s′ .

86

Definition 4.3. For functions ρ and τ satisfying (4.1), ρ is called a τ -reduction
of problem q to problem p.16

4.4.6 Interpretations of Reductions
The usefulness and strength of a reduction is determined by two factors:
• by how much ρ blows up the complexity of a solver s (i.e., reduces the
efficiency), and
• by how much τ reduces the performance. (In some cases, τ can also increase the performance.)
Inequality (4.1) can be interpreted in two different ways: as implying a lower
bound on q (in terms of p ), or as implying an upper bound on p (in terms of q ).
For the latter interpretation it is convenient to write the inequality in a different
form, by considering a ≤-respecting function λ : Ωq → Ωp satisfying
id ≤ λ τ

(4.2)

(i.e., a ≤ λτ a for all a ∈ Ωq ). If Ωp and Ωq are intervals of R and if τ is strictly
≤-respecting, then one can choose λ = τ −1 (i.e., id = λ τ ). We have
τ p ≤ qρ

=⇒
=⇒

λτ p ≤ λqρ
p ≤ λqρ,

(4.3)

where in the first step we have used the ≤-respecting property of λ. If equality
holds in (4.2), then (4.3) and (4.1) are (logically) equivalent. Inequality (4.3) is the
form of a reduction statement used to state an upper bound on the performance
of any solver for p , in terms of the function q .

4.4.7 Complexity-Theoretic Interpretation
This subsection is not needed for understanding the rest of the course.
Recall the discussion of Section 4.4.1 about complexity. Let us assume that
all problems we consider have the same solcer set Σ and that we have fixed a
computational model with a complexity notion assigning to every solver s ∈ Σ
a complexity value γ(s) in some domain Γ of complexity values, for example
Γ = N. Here γ(s) is the implementation complexity when s is optimally implemented in the computational model. We can then associate with a complexity
value c the class Σc of all solvers with at most that complexity:

Σc = s | γ(s) ≤ c .
16 The term “reduction” is motivated by the fact that, by virtue of the reduction, the problem of

finding a solver for q is “reduced” to the problem of finding a solver for p.

87

We also consider a reduction ρ : Σ → Σ.
Now, one particular interpretation of abstract reductions is that, formally,
the (derived) solver set Σ′ is the set Γ of complexity values, and the (derived)
performance function p ′ is a function Γ → Ω mapping complexities to performance and assigning to a complexity value c the best achievable performance
for any solver with complexity at most c:

p ′ (c) = sup p (s) | s ∈ Σc .

Moreover, the (derived) reduction function ρ ′ is a function Γ → Γ mapping
complexities to complexities. A complexity value c is mapped to the maximal
possible complexity of a solver resulting from applying the reduction ρ to any
solver with complexity at most c:

ρ ′ (c) = sup γ(ρ (s)) | s ∈ Σc .

We do not make this more formal but point out that a computational model
would have to come with an exact description of the function ρ ′ . This would
mean, among other things, that the maximal complexity of an algorithm A with
complexity c invoking an algorithm B with complexity c′ would need to be defined as a function of c and c′ . This is very tedious, and to some extent arbitrary,
already for simple computational models. The use of asymptotics allows to
eliminate the arbitrariness to some extent, but it comes at the (high) price of a
loss of concreteness and significance.

4.4.8 Composition of Reductions
Reductions can be composed:
Lemma 4.5. τ p ≤ q ρ ∧ τ ′ q ≤ r ρ ′ =⇒ τ ′ τ p ≤ r ρ ′ ρ .
Proof. Composing both sides of τ p ≤ q ρ with (the ≤-respecting function) τ ′
on the left side results in τ ′ τ p ≤ τ ′ q ρ since τ ′ is ≤-respecting. Composing
both sides of τ ′ q ≤ r ρ ′ with ρ on the right side results in τ ′ q ρ ≤ r ρ ′ ρ .17
Combining the two inequalities yields τ ′ τ p ≤ r ρ ′ ρ .
Reduction statements of the form (4.3) also compose:
Lemma 4.6. p ≤ λ q ρ ∧ q ≤ λ′ r ρ ′ =⇒ p ≤ λ λ′ r ρ ′ ρ .

4.5 Basic Types of Computational Problems
In this section we discuss the three basic types of computational problems important in cryptography: games, distinction problems, and bit-guessing problems, as instantiations of the abstract problem/solver concepts of the previous
section. We recall Section 2.3 where these problem types were already discussed.

4.5.1 Games and Multi-games
To instantiate the concept of a game, we consider a setting with a set G of (deterministic) games and a set W of (deterministic) winners, and a function
ω: W × G → {0, 1},
where 0 and 1 denote the Boolean truth values, and ω(w, g) = 1 is interpreted as
w winning game g.19
Definition 4.5. A game G is a G-valued random variable over G, a winner W
is a W-valued random variable,20 the performance set is Ω = [0, 1], and the
performance of W for game G is the winning probability, i.e.,

G (W ) := PrW G ω(W, G) = 1 .

We have already seen several examples of games, for example the function
inversion problem or the DL problem.
Multi-games. A more general setting is when a game consists of several (say k)
subgames, i.e., when there are k functions ω1 , . . . , ωk with
ωi : W × G → {0, 1},

4.4.10 Worst-Case Problems

where ωi (w, g) = 1 is interpreted as w winning the i-th subgame of g. Such a
game g (or the probabilistic counter-part G) is called a multi-game.21

For a set P of problems one can define the corresponding worst-case problem,
which is formally again a problem and denotes as P.

Example 4.10. We recall the hash-then-sign example discussed in Chapter 2,
which can be described as a multi-game with three winning conditions:

Definition 4.4. For a set P of problems with the same solver set Σ and the same
performance set Ω, where Ω ⊆ R, the worst-case problem, denoted also as P, is
the problem whose performance function P : Σ → R is defined as follows: the
performance of a solver s is the worst performance for any problem in P:
P (s) = inf p (s).

4.7 Basic Reduction Types

4.7.2 Reductions by a Converter

We consider two simple types of reduction functions ρ for discrete game winners and distinguishers: reductions by a converter and reductions by multiple
invocation.

4.7.1 Multiple Independent Instantiation and Cloning
We introduce two types of operations on random variables: multiple independent copies and multiple clones. These operations can be applied to probabilistic systems, understood as random variables over a set of (deterministic)
systems.
Definition 4.9. For an X -random variable X, we denote by X q the X q random variable consisting of q independent copies of X. More precisely,
X q = (X1 , . . . , Xq ) where X1 , . . . , Xq are independent and each Xi has the same
(marginal) probability distribution as X. Moreover, we denote by hXi the X ∞ random variable consisting of a countable number independent copies of X.24
Definition 4.10. For an X -random variable X, we denote by X [q] the X q random variable consisting of q clones of X. More precisely, X [q] = (X1 , . . . , Xq )
where X1 = · · · = Xq and each Xi has the same (marginal) probability distribution as X.
Example 4.11. If X is a binary random variable with uniform distribution, then
X q is the random variable with uniform distribution over {0, 1}q , and X [q]
is the {0, 1}q -random variable which takes on the two values (0, 0, . . . , 0) and
(1, 1, . . . , 1) with probability 21 .
We will use these two concepts for the case where the random variables are
discrete probabilistic systems (systems, environments, or converters). If S is a
probabilistic system, then we denote by Sq the parallel composition of q independent copies of S, and by S[q] the parallel composition of q clones of S which
all behave identically. If C is a (probabilistic) converter, then Cq is the q-fold
instantiation of C, and we have Cq Sq = (CS)q . If E is a (probabilistic) environment, then Eq is the q-fold instantiation of E, where the order of outputs given

Recall Definition 3.8. The following holds for the deterministic or the probabilistic case. A discrete ((U, V), (X , Y))-converter c transforms a (X , Y)-DDS s into
a (U, V)-DDS cs. This also holds for discrete games g as we view the monotone
binary output as not being seen or affected by the system connected to g. In
other words, if g is a game, then cg is also a game.
A converter c can also be understood as a transformation of a winner w into
another winner, denoted wc, where we have
ω(wc, g) = ω(w, cg).

(4.9)

One can view a (possibly probabilistic) converter C as a reduction function ρ
mapping probabilistic winners to probabilistic winners. We denote this function
as ρC :
ρC : W 7→ ρC (W) = WC.
Using (4.9), we thus have the following trivial reduction equality:
CG = G ρC ,

(4.10)

where on the left side the converter C and system G are composed to form a
game, and on the right side the functions G and ρC are composed as functions.

4.7.3 Reductions by Multiple Instantiation
The second type of reduction is by mutliple invocation of a solver, for example
a winner W. The reduction function ρ mapping W to Wq is denoted as σ q , i.e.,
σ q : W 7→ σ q (W) = Wq .

4.8 Some Reduction Statements for Games
4.8.1 Performance Amplification for Games by Repetition

However, we can think of an infinite sequence as standing for the (infinite) sequence of finite-length
prefixes, which means that we consider an infinite sequence of discrete random experiments. This
should not cause confusion in the rest of the course.

A natural idea for amplifying the performance of a game winner W for a game
G is to repeat it independently for the same instance of the problem, hoping that
one of the attempts will be successful. We saw in Exercise 4.1 that performance
amplification by repetition is not guaranteed to work, i.e., the performance is
not necessarily greater than for a single attempt. Nevertheless, repetition is one

94

24 Formally, when considering random variables over X ∞ , one leaves the realm of discrete prob-

ability theory since X ∞ is not countable. The formal treatment then becomes more cumbersome.

of the most important reduction techniques. However, as such, it only works
for independent instances (see below).
Let ψq (x) denote the probability that among q independent binary random
variables, each of which is 1 with probability x, at least one of them is 1. Moreover, let χq : [0, 1] → [0, 1] denote the inverse function, i.e., χq = ψq−1 :

ψq (x) := 1 − (1 − x)q

4.8.2 Cloning Game Systems
In many reductions one needs multiple copies of the same instance of a game.
For a search problem it is obvious what it means to copy an instance if it is
assumed to be represented as a bit-string. In contrast, for general games, which
can be systems with state, it may not even be possible to repeat the game in the
same initial configuration. This is possible only if one can clone a given instance
of a game.

If Wq is connected to Gq , this corresponds to q independent copies of W connected to G, i.e., Wq Gq = (WG)q . The q MBO’s of the q copies of G are hence

The above reduction only works for independent copies of G, i.e., for Gq ∨ .
What one actually wants is to amplify the performance for a given instance g of
a game. In order for this to work, a game must have two properties: it must be
clonable and it must be self-reducible, two concepts discussed in the next two
subsections.
95

Recall the notion of equivalence of probabilistic systems (Definition 3.19). If
two systems are equivalent, they behave identically in any environment, i.e.,
they generate the same transcript distribution. In particular, for any winner W,
the winning probability of two equivalent games G and H is the same: G (W) =
H (W), or in short: G = H . This implies

4.8.3 Random Self-Reduction of Games
Definition 4.13. A probabilistic game G defined as a probability distribution
over a set G of DDS (the instance set) is called random self-reducible by converter
R if
∀g ∈ G : Rg ≡ G.
The following lemma states that for a random game G that is random selfreducible by R, an average-case winner can be transformed by the reduction
function ρR into a worst-case winner with the same success probability.
25 Note that K depends on q, but to simplify the notation we do not make this explicit.

4.8.4 Combining Clonability and Random Self-Reduction
We now show that random self-reducibility and clonability of a game G can be
used to amplify the performance of a winner.
Theorem 4.10. If game G is random self-reducible by R and q-clonable by K, then
ψq G = G ρK σ q ρR .
Proof. If G is random self-reducible by R, then

efficiency of a winner W, then if no efficient winner can win G with probability
p, this implies that no efficient winner can win G with probability χq (p). In
view of (4.11), this probability χq (p) can be made arbitrarily small, even for p
very close to 1, by an appropriate choice of q. There is a trade-off: increasing the
parameter q makes the upper bound of the theorem stronger (i.e., smaller), but
on the other hand makes Wq Rq K less efficient.
From this statement one can obtain the following asymptotic statement as a
corollary: If it is computationally hard to solve G with probability more than
β (for any β, e.g. β = 0.999) then it is even hard to achieve any non-negligible
success probability. In other words, a weak intractability assumption provably
implies a strong one.
Example 4.12. The DL problem in group H = hhi is random self-reducible and
efficiently clonable. The converter R takes an instance y = hx as input (at the
right interface), chooses r ∈ {0, . . . , |H| − 1} at random, outputs y ′ = y · hr at
the left interface, and for the (attempted) solution w at the left interface outputs
w − r at the right interface.
This randomizer system R is correct since the value hr and hence also y ′ = y · hr
is a uniformly random group element and because w is a solution for y ′ if and
only if w − r is a solution for y.

4.9 Hardness Amplification for Games
4.9.1 Combining Several Games
One can combine several independent games G1 , . . . , Gk into a single game by
requesting that all games be won. Recall Definition 4.6 and that this game is
denotes as
[G1 , . . . , Gk ]∧ .
Example 4.13. Recall the function inversion game for a function f . Given several functions (which could be the same), one can define the combined game of
inverting all the functions for independent uniformly random inputs. This is
equivalent to inverting the direct product of all functions on a uniformly random input.

4.9.2 The Goal of Hardness Amplification
The reduction function in Theorem 4.10 is ρK σ q ρR , and hence the theorem
states that for any winner W for G, the winning probability of ρK σ q ρR (W) =
Wq Rq K for G is strongly amplified, namely by the function ψq .
We briefly discuss the consequences of this result in terms of asymptotic
statements. If the reduction function ρK σ q ρR is considered as preserving the
97

We want to investigate the following apparently simple statement:
If one cannot win game G with probability better than β and one cannot win
game H with probability better than γ, then, if G and H are independent, one
cannot win both games with probability better than βγ.
98

This statement is trivially true for games corresponding to search problems
if we consider computationally unbounded winners.26 If β is the probability
that G can be won and γ is the probability that H can be won, then βγ is the
probability that both games can be won.
However, if we consider computationally bounded winners, things are substantially more involved. One can say that G is hard above β (denoted hard (G, β),
and possibly defined precisely later), if no efficient winner has a winning probability of non-negligibly more than β. Then the statement we want to prove is:
If G is hard above β and H is hard above γ, then [G, H]∧ is hard above βγ:
hard (G, β) ∧ hard (H, γ) =⇒ hard ([G, H]∧ , βγ).
The proof of this apparently simple statement is not as easy as it may seem,
and it holds only for clonable games. One has to be careful about what this
statement means, i.e., what needs to be proved. It means the following:
If there exists an efficient βγ-winner for [G, H]∧ , then there exists either an efficient β-winner for G or an efficient γ-winner for H.
A constructive proof of this statement shows how one can construct, given
any efficient βγ-winner W for [G, H]∧ , either an efficient β-winner for G or an
efficient γ-winner for H.
Before we state the theorem and the proof, we need a lemma.

Note that µ1 is a function S → [0, 1] and µ2 is a function T → [0, 1]. We have

4.9.3 A Lemma on Multi-Argument [0, 1]-Valued Functions
In this section we state a general lemma on functions X → [0, 1]. Such a function, say µ : X → [0, 1], can be interpreted as follows. We consider a random

instance.

4.10 Relating Games and Distinction Problems

Hardness amplification can be generalized from k = 2 to a general number k
of games, i.e., to the game [G1 , . . . , Gk ]∧ . Lemma 4.11 can be generalized as
follows, where for simplicity we let all ǫ’s be equal.

Recall Section 4.6 (and hence also Section 3.7), where discrete games, distinction problems, and bit-guessing problems were introduced. In this section we
investigate relations between discrete games and distinction problems. This is
important because it is generally easier to show the hardness of a game than
the hardness of a distinction problem, and the transition between the two is
required if one wants to prove a statement about indistinguishabilty, as for example in constructive cryptography, based on assumptions about the hardness
of one or several games.
As explained in Section 3.7, a game G, a winner W, an object S, and a distinguisher D are probabilistic discrete systems. The statements we make in this
section depend only on the behavior of these systems (see Definition 3.19), i.e.,
they are not affected by which element of the equivalence class of systems with
the same behavior is considered. We can therefore understand a statement about
(system) behavior as a statement holding for any specific probabilstic system
with that behavior.

103

104

λ(x, y) := (1 + δ)xy + δ ′
′

′

for ρ 1 (W) = Wq Hq K and ρ 2 (W) = Wq Gq L, where q and q ′ are as described.
The theorem has two parameters, δ and δ ′ , which one wants to choose as
small as possible. Examining (4.33), we note that δ ′ is the more critical parameter
since q and q ′ are proportional to its inverse, while the dependence of q and q ′
on δ is only logarithmic.

by making dummy queries. In the analysis we therefore consider transcripts
(X q , Y q ) of q inputs and outputs rather than transcripts of variable length.

4.11.2 From Conditional Equivalence to Indistinguishability
In this section we show that conditional equivalence can be used to prove indistinguishability statements in a simple manner, by showing that one can restrict
the analysis to non-adaptive distinguishers.
Definition 4.20. For a game S we define bS as the game system S for which the
outputs Yi are blocked, i.e., b is the simple converter that is transparent for the
queries Xi but blocks the replies Yi .
To win game bS means to win game S blindly, without seeing the outputs. Equivalently, this means to win the game non-adaptively since the inputs
x1 , . . . , xq can be interpreted as being chosen in advance, before seeing any outputs. The best probability in winning a game S non-adaptively (i.e., Γ(bS)) is
generally lower than the best probability in winning it adaptively (i.e., Γ(S)).
Of interest for us is that proving a bound on the best winning probability is
generally much easier for bS.

110

4.11.3 Example: The URP-URF “Switching Lemma”
We discuss a simple result which is in the literature usually called the PRP-PRF
“switching lemma”, where PRF (PRP) stands for pseudo-random function (permutation). But it is independent of complexity-theoretic arguments and is simply the statement that a URP Pn is indistinguishable from a URF Rn,n unless
the number of queries is close to 2n/2 , which is exponential and generally infeasible. This means that whenever one needs a pseudo-random function (PRF)
one can also use a pseudo-random permutation (PRP) without losing much security. Since a block cipher (like AES) is often assumed to be a PRP, it can also
be used as a PRF, as is for instance the case in the CBC-MAC.
We need the following definition and lemma.

Chapter 5

Constructive Cryptography

Definition 4.22. The probability that a set of q independent and uniformly chosen values from an alphabet of size t contains a value twice (a collision) is denoted as pcoll (t, q).
Lemma 4.18. pcoll (t, q) ≤ 12 q 2 /t.

5.1 The Construction Paradigm

Proof. The proof is left as an exercise.

In this chapter we discuss selected aspects of a theory, called constructive cryptography, that was initiated in 20111 and which is still under development.

Recall that [q]S denotes the system S limited to q queries.
Lemma 4.19. ∆([q]Rn,n , [q]Pn ) ≤ 21 q 2 2−n .
The lemma can also be stated as
h[q]Rn,n | [q]Pn i ≤ 21 q 2 2−n .
Proof. As shown in Example 4.15, we can define an MBO for the system Rn,n ,
resulting in the game R̂n,n , where the MBO is 1 if and only if for some distinct
inputs the outputs are equal (a collision). We have R̂n,n |≡ Pn (see Example 4.15)
and hence, according to Theorem 4.17, we obtain
∆([q]Rn,n , [q]Pn ) ≤ Γ(b[q]R̂n,n ).
It remains to analyze Γ(b[q]R̂n,n ). An optimal strategy is to choose the input
sequence xq to b[q]R̂n,n with (arbitrary) distinct values. For any such choice of
xq , the probability of causing a collision is pcoll (2n , q), hence Lemma 4.18 can be
applied.

5.1.1 Modular Construction of Complex Systems
A central paradigm in any constructive discipline, for example in software design, the construction of machines, the definition of communication protocols,
and also in security engineering and cryptography, is to construct a powerful
complex system from simpler component systems (e.g. called modules), which
each may consist of yet simpler component systems, and so on. The technical
meaning of the term “construction” differs from context to context.
For example, the purpose of a software component is to correctly implement
a system from given library modules. Similarly, the purpose of applying errorcorrecting codes can be seen as constructing a reliable, error-free communication channel from an unreliable (error-prone or noisy) channel. In the context
of cryptography, the purpose of encryption can be seen as constructing a secure communication channel from an authenticated channel and a secret key, as
already briefly discussed in Section 2.4.
Such a construction can be represented as a tree.2 There are two dual and
equivalent interpretations of the iterative construction process leading to this
tree: top-down and bottom-up. In a top-down interpretation one starts from the
final object, the root, and in each step one replaces a leaf in the current tree by
a subtree, by replacing the object at that leaf by a construction of it from lower1 The first paper was: U. Maurer and R. Renner, Abstract Cryptography, The Second Symposium in
Innovations in Computer Science, ICS 2011, Tsinghua University Press, pp. 1–21, Jan. 2011.
2 More generally, it could be a directed acyclic graph.

111

112

level objects. This paradigm is sometimes called step-wise refinement, especially
in software design. In a bottom-up interpretation one starts from a list of (parallel) resources corresponding to the leafs in the tree and subsequently constructs
the resources at higher-level nodes in the tree, until one arrives at the root. In
both interpretations, each inner node in the tree corresponds to a construction
step.
In order for this modular construction approach to be useful in a context
where one wants to prove a property of the overall construction (corresponding
to the entire tree), it should be sufficient to prove the property locally, for each
node in the tree. The modular approach can be called sound if the property holds
globally, provided it holds locally, i.e., if one can use an induction proof on the
tree. This can also be called composition.
In the following we treat the concept of composable constructions at the most
abstract level. The treatment in this section is not specific to cryptography and
applies in principle to any constructive discipline. Our goal is to capture the
general notion of a construction, i.e., what is common to the various notions of
a construction.

5.1.2 Abstract Constructions
Two types of objects are involved in constructions: the objects one constructs,
which we call resources (or resource specifications, and the methods by which one
performs a construction, which we call constructors. If resource S can be constructed from resource R by means of constructor a, then we write
a

R −→ S.
Typically, S and R are specifications of systems (see below) and a is a constructor that is guaranteed (or assumed) to transform any system satisfying specification R into one satisfying specification S.
More generally, a constructor can make use of several resources R1 , . . . , Rm ,
which can be understood as a single resource, denoted [R1 , . . . , Rm ], and we can
write
a
[R1 , . . . , Rm ] −→ S.

5.1.3 Composition
Constructors can be composed: If S is constructed by a from R and T is constructed by b from S, then the two constructors a and b can be composed to
obtain a new constructor, denoted as a ◦ b. Soundness of the construction approach means that
a

b

a◦b

R −→ S ∧ S −→ T =⇒ R −→ T.

(5.1)

This is a property that a construction notion does not automatically satisfy; it
must be proved for a given definition of a construction notion.
Definition 5.2. A construction −→ for Ω and Γ is called composable if (5.1) holds.
More generally, we assume that a parallel composition (or tuple-building)
operation is defined which allows to view several resources R1 , . . . , Rm as a
single resource denoted [R1 , . . . , Rm ]. One can also require that

5.2 Resource Specifications, Constructions, and Relaxations

We capture the above in the following definition:
Definition 5.1. A construction for a resource set Ω and a constructor set Γ is a

5.2.1 Resource Specifications
We consider a set Φ of objects, called resources, and let the set Ω of the previous
section be3 Ω = P(Φ). In the context of this course, resources are probabilistic
3 Recall that for a set A, P(A) denotes the power set of A, i.e., the set of subsets of A.

discrete systems (PDS), see Section 5.3, but in this section they can be seen as

Definition 5.3. A resource specification is a subset of Φ. If R ∈ Φ satisfies R ∈ R
we say that R satisfies specification R. For two specifications R and S, if R ⊆ S
we say that R is (at least) as specific as S.
Let R ⊆ S. For a given resource R, it is more useful to know that R ∈ R than
to know that R ∈ S. However, it is often useful to replace a given specification
R by a weaker specification S if S is easier to understand and analyse. We can
call S an abstraction of R.

5.3 Constructions for Discrete Systems
In this section we consider the special case of the previous section where the
resource set Φ is the set of probabilistic discrete systems (PDS, see Section 3.5),
and constructions are defined as application one or more converters.

5.3.1 Interface Types
We consider three different types of interfaces of resources:
• Party interfaces: model the access that regular parties have to the resource.
• Adversary interfaces: model the access that malicious entities have to the
resource. Typically we consider a single adversarial interface, often denoted by E (for Eve).
• Free interfaces: model the access that the environment has to the resource.
Example 5.1. An authenticated communication channel can be modeled as having two party-interfaces, A and B, an adversary interface E, as well as a free
interface F . Messages can be input at A, forwarded by a trigger input at F , and
retrieved at B (if previously forwarded). Messages input at A can be retrieved
at E (without need for forwarding). The forwarding at F models that a channel
need not be reliable. The idea is that in a channel (e.g. a secure channel) constructed from the authenticated channel, the message is retrievable by B exactly
if this is the case in the assumed authenticated channel.

5.3.2 Constructions
A construction is defined by a tuple of converters, one for each party (interface).
Such a tuple of converters is often called a protocol.
Definition 5.8. A protocol for a set P = {P1 , . . . , Pn } of parties consists of a converter πi for each party Pi ∈ P, i.e., it is a tuple π = (π1 , . . . , πn ) of converters.
The construction function defined by protocol π = (π1 , . . . , πn ) is
R 7→ π1P1 · · · πnPn R.

The general guiding principle in constructive cryptography is that everything that is considered relevant (for the analysis one wants to perform) is modeled as a resource. In contrast, converters perform only operations considered
to be irrelevant. If, for instance, computing power, memory, or randomness
needed for a cryptographic construction is considered to matter, then it has to
be explicitly modeled as part of the resource. To illustrate this point, we explain
a few possible such explicit choices.
1. The term information-theoretic security is usually used when computation
(at least by the adversary) is irrelevant. In such a case the converter set
includes all systems, regardless of the computational complexity of implementing it.
2. If computing power is considered relevant, then one can consider converters that perform no computation by themselves but only connect systems
and possibly input constants (for example a program). Any computational
or randomness resource is modeled as a resource.
3. If, for some notion of efficiency, efficient computing power is considered
irrelevant, then one can consider converters as efficiently implementable
systems. Typically in cryptography, efficient is defined as some form of
polynomial-time notion, which of course, and unfortunately, requires now
the objects to be defined asymptotically in some way. A main reason for
using polynomial-time is that this notion, if properly defined, is closed
under composition.
We will take the second viewpoint, i.e., that any computation is performed
in a resource. We will not formalize the class Σ of such trivial converters.
For example, if we so far considered a resource system αR consisting of a
converter α attached to system R, then we can understand α as a resource, for
example denoted α̃, which is provided in parallel with R. And we can consider
a trivial converter π that simply connects α̃ and R, i.e., we have
αR = π[R, α̃].
However, now that this viewpoint is explained, we can continue to write αR
rather than π[R, α̃].

5.3.3 Resources vs. Converters

5.3.4 ǫ-Relaxation

Up to now, converters, such as an encryption converter or a decryption converter, were understood as performing some computation (e.g. encrypting a
message). A question that arises is where we draw the boundary between a
resource providing computing power and the computing power of a converter.

As already mentioned earlier, the ǫ-relaxation of a PDS-specification is defined
as follows:

Rǫ = R′ | ∃R ∈ R : ∆(R, R′ ) ≤ ǫ .

117

118

5.3.5 Reduction-based Relaxations

5.3.7 A new Look at the Simulation Paradigm

While ǫ-relaxations capture information-theoretic closeness, we also need to
capture closeness in a “computational sense”. Let f be a performance function
mapping distinguishers to R, involving computational problems p1 , . . . , pn , i.e.,
f is of the form


f = λ p 1 , . . . , p n ρ1 , . . . , ρn

The notion of a simulator is central in cryptography. We recall Section 2.4.3
for an example of a simulator. A simulator is a converter that is used to argue
that what an adversary Eve can achieve in a real-world setting she could also
achieve in an ideal-world setting, by use of the simulator. This means that if one
considers the ideal world adequate (e.g. secure), then so is the real world since
in it, not more can go wrong than in the ideal world. A simulator is used in a
thought experiment, it would never be actually implemented. Nevertheless, it
must be as efficient as possible because it appears in reduction transformations.
More concretely, consider two systems U (real) and V (ideal) with an Einterface (accessible by Eve), and suppose there exists a simulator σ such that

as discussed in Section 4.4.9. We define:

Rf := R′ | ∃R ∈ R : hR | R′ i ≤ f .

(5.5)

For computational closeness, as explained in Chapter 4, the concrete functions ρi and λ matter. The ρi should increase the complexity of a distinguisher
as little as possible, and λ should be as small as possible.6
Exercise 5.1. Explain iterated PRG-expansion as a construction of a relaxed version of a (system outputting a) long uniform random string from (system outputting a) short uniform random string .
We do not discuss compatability properties of this relaxation type. This issue
is a bit subtle.

U ≡ σ E V.
This can be interpreted as follows. For any converter α that Eve might want to
apply in the real world (i.e., she would form αE U), there exists a converter β
that Eve could apply in the ideal world (i.e., she would form β E V), such that
αE U = β E V, i.e.,
∀α ∃β αE U ≡ β E V.
The simulator is a proof technique to prove this statement. Namely, the simulator provides the translation from α to β: α 7→ β = ασ:
β E V ≡ (ασ)E V = αE (σ E V) ≡ αE U.

5.3.6 ∗-Relaxations
We recall that an adversary entity Eve is only a hypothetical entity, taken into
consideration in order to state guarantees to the honest parties no matter what
Eve does. Eve simply stands for a universal quantifier (∀) over strategies, not
for an entity that actually participates. In other words, we want to capture that
there is no guarantee for what happens at an adversary interface. An arbitrary
converter could be applied at such an interface. This is modeled by the following relaxation type.
Definition 5.9. The ∗-relaxation of a specification R for interface E is:
∗E

R


:= αE R | α ∈ Σ, R ∈ R ,

where Σ denotes the set of trivial converters mentioned above. If there is only a
single adversary interface, we can drop the superscript E and simply write R∗ .
We point out that ∗-relaxation is compatible according to Definitions 5.6
and 5.7.
6 In asymptotic terms, if the ρ are efficiency-preserving and λ is negligibility-preserving, then,
i
if all problems P1 , . . . , Pn are hard, then the distinction problem hS | Ti is hard, i.e., S and T are
computationally indistinguishable.

119

We can now take a new look at the simulation paradigm. As discussed in
Section 5.3.3, a simulator σ can be seen as a resource σ̃ available to the adversary.
We can write
σ E V = σ ′E [V, σ̃E ] ∈ [V, σ̃E ]∗ .
with a trivial converter σ ′ ∈ Σ appropriately connecting V and σ̃. Hence we
have
U ≡ σ E V =⇒ U∗ ⊆ [V, σ̃E ]∗ ,
where we need that the converter set contains the identity element, i.e., U ∈ U∗ .
A classical cryptographic statement involving a simulator can hence be understood as a statement about ∗-relaxations.

5.3.8 Game-Relaxation
Definition 5.10. For a system T, enhanced with an MBO to a game T̂, the gamerelaxation of T̂, denoted T̂⊢ , is the set of PDS that behave as T as long as the
MBO is 0 and behave arbitrarily once the MBO is 1.7

5.3.9 Substitution Relaxation
Substitution relaxations will not be discussed in this course.

5.4 Example: Authentication Amplification
We give an example of a construction statement based on game relaxation.
Suppose one can send a short message authentically (e.g. over the phone)
but needs to send a very long message m authentically over the Internet. How is
this possible? A very natural solution is to hash the message m, to send the hash
value h(m) over the authenticated channel, and to send m over the Internet.
Why is this secure, and which mathematical statement should we use to
capture the security statement?
Informally, the statement one would typically make is: If h is collisionresistant, then the authentication system is secure. However, we already know
that a single function can not be collision-resistant, so this statement does not
make sense. This is why hash functions are often defined as asymptotic families, but we argue that introducing the artefact of asymptotics is the wrong
approach for dealing with the stated problem.
A better statement is: There is an efficient reduction from the collisionfinding problem for h to breaking the authenticaton scheme.
However, we can make an even more precise statement without need for
reductions: If the constructed resource is not an authenticated channel, then a
121

⊆

resource at interface i computing the hash function h once, hsh is the converter applied
by A and chk is the converter applied by B, and γcoll is the converter defining the
collision game at the interface of HE .

5.5 Parameterized Resources and Constructions
Often, a resource R is parameterized by a parameter r, for example the allowed
number of queries, and the parameter ǫ in an ǫ-relaxation or the function f in a
reduction-based relaxation depend on r.
The parameterized restriction of an a priori unbounded resource R is modeled by filters φr (see Section 3.4.3).
Definition 5.11. A parameterized discrete single-interface resource with parameter set Z is a family {φr R}r∈Z of finite resources, where R is a (generally infinite) resource system and {φr }r∈Z is a parameterized family of filter converters.
Example 5.2. Recall that [r] is the special case of a filter restricting access to r
queries. Hence [r]Rn,n is a uniform random function (URF) parameterized by
the number r of queries it allows.
We will make parameterized statements of the following form:

The constructing converter α does not depend on r, i.e., it works for any r, but
α will be such that for each r,

version of S (i.e., ψr S), as long as access (of a distinguisher) is ψr -restricted.

6.1.2 Example: k-wise Independent Random Variables

Chapter 6

Randomness Expansion

An example of the first type are so-called k-wise independent random variables.
Given k uniformly random values in {0, 1}m (for some m) one can construct 2m
values that appear perfectly uniformly random as long as one sees (i.e., queries)
at most k of them. This corresponds to a function table {0, 1}m → {0, 1}m chosen
according to some distribution. Such a construction can be achieved by letting
the k random values be the coefficients a0 , . . . , ak−1 of a polynomial
a(x) = ak−1 xk−1 + · · · + a1 x + a0

In this chapter we look at a number of constructions of single-interface resources, primarily the problem of expanding randomness. We omit the discussion of pseudo-randomness expansion.

6.1 The Randomness Expansion Problem
6.1.1 Introduction
Randomness is the most important resource in cryptography, especially secret
randomness shared by some entities and not known to the other entities. An
important paradigm in cryptography is to expand a given amount of randomness into a larger amount of randomness, for example to expand a secret key
into several secret keys or to construct a uniform random function (URF) from
a short uniform random value.
In a context where several parties need to perform the expansion operation
(as in the key expansion problem mentioned above), the expansion must be deterministic. Deterministic computation can of course not really expand the given
entropy, it can only expand the appearance of entropy. This can be achieved in
two different ways:
• Information-theoretic: The expanded randomness is indistinguishable
from true randomness even for computationally unbounded distinguishers, but there is a restriction on the distinguisher’s access (e.g. the number
of queries). See Section 6.2.
• Computational: The expanded randomness is only pseudo-random, i.e.,
computationally indistinguishable from true randomness.
Randomness expansion can in our framework be understood as a construc123

over GF (2m ) of degree k − 1, and considering the 2m values a(x) for all x ∈
GF (2m ), i.e., considering the function
x 7→ a(x).

The proof that this works is left as an exercise. Stated as a construction, we have,
for any k:1
[k]α

Ukm −→ [k]Rm,m ,
where α is the converter that fetches the km-bit string at the inside (right interface) and continuously takes inputs at the outside (left interface) and for each
such input x returns a(x) at the outside.

6.2 Domain Extension of Uniform Random Functions
6.2.1 Motivation
In the above example, we have expanded k uniform random m-bit strings into
2m m-bit strings which are uniformly random as long as one only accesses t values, where t ≤ k. Intuitively, t can not be greater than k if information-theoretic
security is required. Therefore, if such an expansion should work against an adversary who can ask a huge number of queries, then one must already assume
to be given a large amount of randomness, for example a URF Rm,n which constitutes an exponential amount of randomness.
However, since the construction should be efficient, each query to the constructed randomness resource should involve only few of the given random
values. Therefore the above polynomial-based construction does not work because each query involves the entire given randomness. We need an alternative
approach.
1 Note that the resource U
km is considered to be read in one shot and therefore no access restriction must be modeled. Alternatively, one could model it as a URF {0, . . . , k − 1} → {0, 1}m .

124

6.2.2 Variable Input-Length URFs
Cryptographic primitives, like a pseudo-random function (e.g. a block cipher),
often have fixed input length. In applications (e.g. a message authentication
code or an encryption function), one often needs a primitive with larger input
length. Often the input length is not bounded, i.e., the input alphabet of a system is {0, 1}∗. Such a system is usually referred to as variable input-length (VIL).
Definition 6.1. The term variable input-length URF (a VIL-URF) refers to the behavior of a ({0, 1}∗ , {0, 1}n)-system which for each new input in {0, 1}∗ outputs
a fresh uniformly random value in {0, 1}n, and which replies consistently if inputs are repeated. This system (behavior) is denoted as Vn .2

The security depends on how the block former within CBC is defined. If
the CBC-MAC is used for messages of variable length, then the encoding of the
message must be prefix-free, for example by prepending the message with an
extra block encoding the message length. In other words, it must be guaranteed
that for no two distinct messages m1 and m2 , the resulting block sequence (at
the output of the block former) for m1 is a prefix of the block sequence for m2 .
For stating the following theorem we need to define a suitable restriction
converter θr for Vn , as follows. For a fixed block-former, for each message θr
determines the number of blocks the block-former outputs (this is roughly the
total length, in blocks, of the messages) and keeps track of the total number of
such blocks resulting for all messages seen so far. When this number exceeds r,
then θr stops replying to queries. Simply stated, we have3

An important practical goal in cryptography is to construct a VIL-URF Vn
from a URF Rn,n by a deterministic converter α:
ψr α

[r]Rn,n −→

ψr Vn

fr

for appropriate ψr and fr . In this section we discuss two approaches to this
problem. The restriction ψr will correspond roughly to a restriction on the total
length of all inputs.
In practice, this construction is composed with a (computationally secure)
construction of Rn,n .

6.2.3 CBC-MAC as a Randomness Expander
A MAC scheme should allow to authenticate messages of arbitrary length. A
completely random function Vn would be an ideal MAC for which the cheating probability is always 2−n , independently of how many queries have been
asked. Many MAC-functions are defined as a hashing process that makes use
of a round function F which is assumed to behave like a URF Rn,n . A typical
implementation of F is by use of a block cipher, for example AES, for a uniform
secret key. If AES denotes the converter that takes at the right (inside) interface
as input a 128-bit string (a key U128 ) and at the left (outside) interface answers
encryption queries, then we have F = AES U128 .
A frequently used MAC is the so-called CBC-MAC (see Figure 2.9), which
has also received considerable attention as an example for doing cryptographic
security proofs. Let CBC denote the converter implementing the CBC-MAC, i.e.,
accepting messages and returning the MAC at the outside and making calls to
the system in our case a URF Rn,n or F) at the inside (right interface). For each
(new) message, CBC applies a block former to the message and then digests the
obtained block sequence block by block, each time invoking the system at its
right interface. The initial value of the state is a fixed and known parameter.

6.2.4 A Construction Based on the Collision-Game for a Compression Function
We describe a construction of Vn from Rn,n based on the game of provoking a
collision at the output of a system S that compresses input messages to n bits.
Here S can be the evaluation of a hash function (see Section 6.2.5) or a system
(involving a secret key) for which one can prove an information-theoretic bound
on the probability of provoking a collision (see Section 6.2.6).
Recall from Definition 3.11 that for an (X , Y)-system S and a (Y, Z)-system
T, S ⊲ T denotes the cascade of the two systems, which is an (X , Z)-system. We
can write this cascade as
casc[S, T],
as discussed in Section 3.4.4, where casc ∈ Σ is a converter.
127

For an ({0, 1}∗, {0, 1}n)-system S, the system casc[S, Rm,n ] is a
({0, 1}∗, {0, 1}n)-system. If S is a random function, i.e., a random variable
over (a finite subset of) functions {0, 1}∗ → {0, 1}n, then also casc[S, Rm,n ] is a
random function.
Intuitively, as long as no collision (equal output values) for distinct input
values) occurs at the output of S, the output of casc[S, Rm,n ] is uniformly random for every fresh input. Hence casc[S, Rm,n ] behaves as Vn as long as no
collision occurs. The game of provoking an output collision in S is denoted as
Ŝ.4

like D, except that all inputs to D′ are (replaced by) fresh uniformly random
(but consistent) values. Moreover, D′ ’s winning probability is of course upper
bounded by the winning probability for Ŝ of the best distinguisher (or winner).

128

6.2.5 Using a Collision-Resistant Hash Function
If S implements a hash function h, then Ŝ is the collision-finding game for h.
Theorem 6.2 states that any distinguisher D for systems [r]casc[S, Rn,n ] and
[r]Vn can be simply transformed into a (non-adaptive) collision finder D′ with
the same performance.
This is an example of a reduction statement for which there exists no corresponding hardness statement. One would be tempted to make the following
statement: If h is collision-resistant (i.e., no efficient algorithm can find a collision for h with non-negligible probability), then casc[S, Rm,n ] and Vn are computationally indistinguishable (i.e., no efficient distinguisher can distinguish
casc[S, Rm,n ] and Vn with non-negligible advantage).5 However, this argument
is flawed because one can not even define collision-resistance for a single function h meaningfully. For every hash function h, there exists an efficient collision
finder, namely one that simply outputs a collision for h (which exists because h
is shrinking).

6.2.6 δ-Almost Universal Hash Functions
Our goal is to make use of Theorem 6.2 to obtain an information-theoretically
secure construction of Vn . For this purpose we need to instantiate S as a system involving a secret key that is provably collision-resistant, in an informationtheoretic sense.
Definition 6.2. Let δ be a function N → R . A function H : K × Y → Z for
Y ⊆ {0, 1}∗ is called a δ-almost universal hash function (δ-AUH for short)6 if, for
any distinct y, y ′ ∈ Y,

Pr HK (y) = HK (y ′ ) ≤ δ(max(|y|, |y ′ |))
+

for uniformly selected key K ∈ K.7

Note that we write the first argument of the function H as a subscript to
indicate its special role.
We now describe a concrete construction of a δ-AUH, for K = Z = {0, 1}m
and Y = {0, 1}∗. An input y ∈ {0, 1}∗ is cut into m-bit blocks, then a 1-bit is
appended, and then between 0 and k − 1 0-bits are appended to complete the
k-bit block.8 The resulting block sequence is
y ′ = [y0 , y1 , . . . , yt−1 ],
5 Note that one does not need to restrict the number of queries by filters in this statement because
a distinguisher can only make at most one query per computation step and the number of steps of
a feasible distinguisher is bounded.
6 This definition is more general than the definition in the literature where δ does not depend on
the length of the input.
7 Here |y| denotes the length of y.
8 Other paddings are also possible.

129

where t is the length of y ′ in blocks, i.e., t = ⌈(|y| + 1)/m⌉.
We consider a fixed representation of the elements of the finite field GF (2m )
(with addition and multiplication operations) as m-bit strings. An input y ∈
{0, 1}∗ can be interpreted as a polynomial py (x) of degree t − 1 over GF (2m ), as
follows: For y, let y ′ = [y0 , y1 , . . . , yt−1 ], as above, and define py (x) as
py (x) := yt−1 xt−1 + yt−2 xt−2 + · · · + y1 x + y0 .

Consider the following function H : {0, 1}m × {0, 1}∗ → {0, 1}m:
HK (y) := py (K).
Lemma 6.3. H as described is a δ-AUH for δ(ℓ) = 2−m ℓ/m.
Proof. Any two polynomials of degree at most d over a field can agree for at most
d values because the difference of the two polynomials has also degree at most
d and hence has at most d roots. (Note that a root of the difference polynomial
corresponds to a point where the two polynomials agree.) Thus the probability,
when choosing K uniformly at random, that a point is selected at which the
two polynomials agree, is at most the degree divided by the field size, 2m . For
ℓ := |y| the degree is ⌈ℓ/m⌉ − 1 which is at most ℓ/m.

efficient than the CBC-MAC construction and could be used as a replacement.

interface provides access to HK . Hence hUk corresponds to a system containing H equipped with a uniform key. Let ĥ be the collision game for h. For
any fixed set of r inputs, each of length at most ℓ, to h Uk , it follows from Definition 6.2 and the union bound (as in Lemma 4.18) that the probability of a

Chapter 7

Constructing Shared Secret

7.1 The Key Agreement Problem
Shared secret keys are a crucial resource in cryptography. Secret keys are not
of primary interest in their own right, i.e., as the final “product” of a security
protocol, but only as a resource used in cryptographic constructions. In a sense,
a secret key is a form of security (e.g. secrecy and authenticity) that can be
conserved and stored for future use. For example, if a secure channel is available
at a certain time t one can use it to transport a secret key which can then later
be used to construct a secure channel at a time t′ > t when only an insecure
channel is available. In a sense this, is comparable to the conservation of solar
energy in the form of ethanol by the use of biological systems (agriculture).
One is interested in constructing shared secret keys from weaker resources,
for example from authenticated communication between A and B.
Definition 7.1. The construction of a shared secret key from authenticated communication by a protocol π = (πA , πB ), for simulator1 σ and a suitable performance function f is called key agreement:

As discussed earlier, the Diffie-Hellman protocol DH = (dhA , dhB ) achieves
this construction, where σ outputs two random group elements, where f =

this means that the total key size is k + k ′ .

7.2 Information-Theoretic Key-Agreement
A general question one asks in cryptography is: Which constructions can be
achieved with information-theoretic (rather than only computational) security? Information-theoretic security means that the constructed resource is an
ǫ-relaxation (for a small ǫ of a target resource specification, and that we do not
care if Eve gets, as part of the constructed resource specification, an arbitrary
resource in Φ (including an arbitrarily powerful computer). This is captured by
including on the right side of a construction the resource specification ΦE .
In Section 7.2.1 we prove that information-theoretically secure key agreement is impossible, which is the reason for resorting to computational security. However, we will mention in Section 7.2.2 that when the model is changed
slightly and an additional resource is assumed, then information-theoretically
secure key agreement is actually possible.

7.2.1 Impossibility
The following theorem follows from Theorem 7.3 below.
Theorem 7.2. There exists no protocol π = (πA , πB ) and simulator σ such that
ǫ

σ E •=====• .

for ǫ ≤ 41 , even if arbitrarily many rounds of authenticated communication are allowed.

This theorem states, in other words, that information-theoretically secure
public-key cryptography is impossible.

random variables X, Y , and Z, respectively, distributed according to some joint
distribution PXY Z .2 If we denote such a resource providing X, Y , and Z as
[PXY Z ], then we consider the construction3

π would trivially achieve the construction. Of course, one is interested in nontrivial cases of this generalized key agreement problem.
We refer the reader to the Appendix where the basics of information theory
are explained. It is sufficient to use the basic information-theoretic inequalities
explained there; no further understanding of information theory is required.
A key-agreement protocol, where A and B initially hold X and Y , respectively, consists of a finite sequence of message exchanges between A and B,
where each message depends on the sender’s complete knowledge at that time.
Let C denote the list of random variables corresponding to the entire communication between A and B. As the last step, A generates a key KA from (X, C)
by a (possibly probabilistic) function, and B generates a key KB from (Y, C). Of
course, one wants that KA = KB with very high probability.
The following theorem states that if H(KA ) substantially exceeds I(X; Y |Z),
then it is unavoidable that either the amount of information the adversary learns
about KA (i.e., I(KA ; ZC)) is substantial, or H(KB |KA ) is substantial, which
implies that KA 6= KB with substantial probability.4
Theorem 7.3. For any key-agreement protocol,
I(KA ; ZC) + H(KA |KB ) ≥ H(KA ) − I(X; Y |Z).
Proof. Let X ′ , Y ′ , and Z ′ be the random variables held by A, B and E, respectively, at some stage of the protocol. We prove that for any next stage of the
protocol,
I(X ′′ ; Y ′′ |Z ′′ ) ≤ I(X ′ ; Y ′ |Z ′ ),

where X ′′ , Y ′′ , and Z ′′ are the random variables held by the parties after the
step is performed. In other words, we prove that the quantity I(X ′ ; Y ′ |Z ′ ) can
not increase under any possible operations that can occur in a protocol. There
are four such operations to consider:
• Generation of local randomness by A (and, analogously, by B). Let R
be a random variable generated by A independently of all other random
variables. Then X ′′ = (X ′ , R), Y ′′ = Y ′ , and Z ′′ = Z ′ , and hence
I(X ′′ ; Y ′′ |Z ′′ ) = I(X ′ R; Y ′ |Z ′ ) = I(X ′ ; Y ′ |Z ′ )
This follows directly from the definition of I(X ′ R; Y ′ |Z ′ ) = H(X ′ RZ ′ ) +
H(Y ′ Z ′ ) − H(X ′ RY ′ Z ′ ) − H(Z ′ ) since the terms H(X ′ Z ′ ) and H(X ′ Y ′ Z ′ )
are both increased by exactly H(R) when R is appended to X ′ .
4 We refer to the Appendix for a brief summary of the basics of information theory. Here we only
need a few very simple properties of the basic information-theoretic quantities, namely:

Fact 7.1. For any random variables X, Y , and Z, we have
1. H(XY ) ≥ H(X) with equality if and only if Y is determined by X,
2. I(X; Y ) ≥ 0,
3. I(X; Y |Z) ≥ 0.

134

• Local computation by A (and, analogously, by B). Let U be computed
from X ′ by some (deterministic) function. Then H(X ′ U ) = H(X ′ ) (or,
equivalently, H(U |X ′ ) = 0). Therefore
I(X ′′ ; Y ′′ |Z ′′ ) = I(X ′ U ; Y ′ |Z ′ ) = I(X ′ ; Y ′ |Z ′ )

since all involved entropies remain unchanged.

It remains to prove the inequality of the theorem from the fact that I(X ′ ; Y ′ |Z ′ )
cannot increase. At the end of a protocol, A and B hold the keys KA and KB
and have discarded the other information, i.e., we have X ′′ = KA and Y ′′ = KB
and Z ′′ = (Z, C). The proof is completed upon noting that

where we have used
H(KA ) − I(KA ; ZC) = H(KA ZC) − H(ZC)
as well as
H(KA KB ZC) − H(KB ZC) = H(KA |KB ZC) ≤ H(KA |KB ).

We state a simple corollary capturing the perfect case.
Corollary 7.4. For any protocol for which KA = KB (= K) with probability 1 and
I(K; ZC) = 0, we have
H(K) ≤ min(I(X; Y ), I(X; Y |Z)).
Proof. KA = KB implies H(KA |KB ) = 0. Since I(KA ; ZC) = I(K; ZC) = 0,
Theorem 7.3 implies that I(X; Y |Z) is an upper bound on H(KA ) = H(K). In
a thought experiment where Eve computes Z ′ = f (Z) for some function f and
deletes Z (replacing it by Z ′ ), the quantity I(X; Y |Z ′ ) is also an upper bound
on H(K) in the original setting (with Z) because Eve could indeed do this. As
a special case, Eve could delete Z entirely. This shows that I(X; Y ) is also an
upper bound on H(K), completing the proof.
Proof of Theorem 7.2. We only give an impossibility proof for the perfect case,
i.e., for ǫ = 0. The proof for small ǫ is similar, but technically a bit more involved.

7.2.3 Privacy Amplification

Section 7.2.2 is not relevant for the exam.
In many realistic settings, the parties A, B, and E can access some information in their environment, which can be interpreted as the initial random
variables X, Y , and Z discussed above. There are reasonable settings in which
I(X; Y |Z) is strictly positive and secret-key agreement by public discussion is
indeed possible. One such setting is the so-called satellite model.
The satellite model: One assumes that a satellite broadcasts random bits that
are received by A, B, and E over independent noisy channels, with bit error
probabilities ǫA , ǫB , and ǫE , respectively. The proof of the following theorem is
beyond the scope of this course.6

Privacy amplification refers to a setting, often arising in information-theoretic
cryptography (including quantum cryptography), where some parties (usually
Alice and Bob) share the value of a random variable W and some party (usually
Eve) knows a random variable V correlated with W . Such a setting is typically
reached after some protocol steps between Alice and Bob, including error correction which guarantees that the values they hold are equal, namely W .
The goal of privacy amplification is for Alice and Bob to generate an essentially uniformly random string K about which Eve has essentially no information, and which can serve as a key. They are allowed to communicate publicly
(though authentically). Typically, privacy amplification is achieved by one party
(say Alice) choosing a random value R, sending R to Bob, and then both Alice
and Bob applying a deterministic publicly-known function ϕ to (W, R):

Theorem 7.5. Secret key-agreement by public discussion is possible in the satellite
model unless it is impossible by Corollary 7.4, i.e., unless either ǫE = 0 or ǫA = 0.5 or
ǫB = 0.5.
In other words, if we denote the satellite setting by SAT, then the construction
ǫ
π
[ •−−−−
→ , ←
−−−−• , SAT ]
−→
σ E •=====• .

is possible for arbitrarily small ǫ and for an adequate π and a simple σ.
We also briefly discuss two other approaches to information-theoretically
secure key agreement:
• Bounded-storage model. In this model one assumes that Alice and Bob
share a short secret key initially and that they want to expand it to a very
long key, secure against an Eve with unbounded computing time, but with
bounded memory. A very long bit-string (called randomizer) R is transmitted from Alice to Bob such that Eve’s memory does not allow to store
the entire value of R (but, say, only 90%). Alice and Bob extract a (long)
secret key from the randomizer, using the initially shared secret key. Security is guaranteed even if Eve can store arbitrary information about the
randomizer string, i.e., she can apply an arbitrary function mapping R to
her memory size. The model has received considerable attention, but its
practicality appears limited.
• Quantum cryptography. Alice and Bob exchange single photons with certain polarizations and then communicate over an authenticated channel to
agree on a secret key. Assuming the laws of quantum physics are correct,
one can prove that Eve, who has full control over the quantum channel
between Alice and Bob, has a choice only between disrupting the process such that Alice and Bob notice this (and can abort), or receiving only
a negligible amount of information about the secret key shared by Alice
and Bob.
6 see U. Maurer, Secret key agreement by public discussion, IEEE Transactions on Information Theory, vol. 39, no. 3, pp. 733–742, 1993.

137

K = ϕ(W, R).
An equivalent formulation, which is usually used, is that Alice selects a function
G at random from a class of compression functions, and that Alice and Bob both
compute K = G(W ).
It is assumed (e.g. known from the analysis of the previous protocol steps)
that there is an upper bound on the uncertainty of W if one knows V . More precisely, the conditional probability distribution PW |V satisfies a certain condition.
Such a condition can be stated in different ways, for example that a condition
on PW |V =v holds with high probability (over values v), or that a condition holds
on average (over values v). Privacy amplification must work even if Alice and
Bob know nothing about PW |V , except that it satisfies the assumed condition.
Example 7.1. Consider a setting where Alice and Bob know a uniformly random
n-bit string W and where Eve is allowed, for a fixed m < n, to (secretly) choose
an arbitrary function f : {0, 1}n → {0, 1}m and to obtain the random variable
V = f (W ). A numerical example is n = 1000 and m = 800, i.e., Eve can obtain
arbitrary 80% of the information about W . Privacy amplification allows Alice
and Bob to obtain a secret key of close to 200 bits (say 180 bits), despite the
fact that which information Eve chose is unknown to Alice and Bob. In other
words, they can essentially harvest all the remaining uncertainty of Eve about
W , without knowing what this uncertainty is. Of course, Eve must choose f
before execution of the privacy amplification protocol.
For simplicity we consider the following simplified description of the setting where Eve’s knowledge V = v is not made explicit. Instead, the probability
distribution PW can be understood as taking Eve’s information already into account (and can be seen as the conditional distribution PW |V =v ). The two views
are equivalent for the following treatment.
For a random variable X over a finite alphabet X we define the following

In privacy amplification statements one often considers that Eve’s knowledge
about W is bounded in terms of min-entropy (i.e, in terms of the maximal probability of W from Eve’s viewpoint). The following lemma shows that an upper

We define a special case of the concept of δ-AUH functions introduced in
Section 6.2.6.
Definition 7.2. A class G of functions A → B is 2-universal (“universal” for short)
if, for any distinct x1 and x2 in A, the probability that g(x1 ) = g(x2 ) is at most
1/|B| when g is chosen at random from G according to the uniform distribution.7
When A = B, the class consisting only of the identity function is trivially a
universal class, although there are others. In all cases, the class of all functions
from A to B is universal, but it is not useful because there are too many functions
in that class. A more useful universal class is that of all linear functions from
{0, 1}n to {0, 1}r . These functions can be described by r×n matrices M over
GF (2), i.e. by rn bits. Another universal class, requiring only n bits to specify a
function, is given in the following lemma.

Lemma 7.8. Let a be an element of GF (2n ) and also interpret x as an element
of GF (2n ). Consider the function {0, 1}n → {0, 1}r assigning to an argument x
the first r bits of the element ax of GF (2n ). The class of all such functions for
a ∈ GF (2n ) is a universal class of functions for 1 ≤ r ≤ n.
Proof. For any two x1 , x2 ∈ GF (2n ) with x1 6= x2 , and for any a ∈ GF (2n ) we
have [ax1 ]r = [ax2 ]r if and only if [a(x1 − x2 )]r = 0r , where [y]r for y ∈ GF (2n )
denotes the first r bits of y. For each of the 2n−r y ∈ {0, 1}n with [y]r = 0r ,
there is exactly one a with a(x1 − x2 ) = y, namely a = y/(x1 − x2 ). (Here
the field property is needed.) Hence, for any x1 6= x2 there are exactly 2n−r
values a for which [ax1 ]r = [ax2 ]r . The probability of choosing such an a is
2n−r /2n = 2−r .
The following theorem implies that if one hashes W down to
r ≪ − log2 pcoll (W )
bits, then not only G(W ), but even the pair (G, G(W )) is essentially uniformly distributed, where the distance from uniform decreases exponentially
in − log2 pcoll (W ) − r. This means that G(W ) is essentially uniform from Eve’s
viewpoint even if G is known to Eve. Therefore the choice of G needs to be
communicated only authentically, but not secretly. This is known as privacy amplification.
Theorem 7.9. Let G be the random variable corresponding to the uniform choice of a
function from a universal class G. Then

7.3 Key-Agreement in the Random Oracle Model
7.3.1 Definition of a Random Oracle
The so-called random oracle model is a useful paradigm for devising and analyzing cryptographic protocols.
Definition 7.3. The term random oracle model (ROM) refers to any cryptographic
scenario in which one assumes the availability of an idealized resource, called
a public random oracle (or simply random oracle), a uniform random function
{0, 1}∗ → {0, 1}k (for some k) accessible9 to all the parties. Such a resource
will be denoted as POk .
The heuristic idea, proposed by Bellare and Rogaway in 1993,10 is to assume
that a cryptographic hash function behaves like a random function. Since it can
be computed by anyone locally, such a hash function can be used as a public
random oracle.
9 Accessible means that each party can evaluate the oracle function for any arguments of its
choice, without leaking information about the arguments.
10 M. Bellare and P. Rogaway, Random oracles are practical: a paradigm for designing efficient
protocols, in V. Ashby, editor, 1st ACM Conference on Computer and Communications Security, pages
62–73. ACM Press, 1993.

141

7.3.2 Key-Agreement Using A TOWP in the ROM
Recall the notion of a trapdoor one-way permutation (TOWP, see Definition 2.18) and RSA as a specific instantiation.
It appears natural to try to achieve the construction
[ •−−−−
→ , ←
−−−−• ]

(σ E •=====• )h

π

−→

for adequate protocol π = (π1 , π2 ) and σ, using a TOWP (which means that
h = G ρ, where ρ is a simple function and G is the inversion game for the
underlying TOWP.
The basic idea is that B generates a parameter-trapdoor pair (p, t) according
to the right distribution, using the corresponding algorithm. The parameter p
is sent over the channel ←
−−−−• . Then A chooses a random key k, computes
y = f (k, p), where f is the corresponding function of the TOWP, and sends y to
B over the channel •−−−−
→ . Using the trapdoor t, B can compute k = g(y, t).
However, this naı̈ve implementation is not secure in general since partial
information about the key might leak to E. For example, for the RSA TOWP,
the value K e (mod n) leaks the information bit whether K is a quadratic residue
modulo n, since this property is preserved by the computation of the e-th power.
This problem can be solved in the ROM, i.e., assuming the availability of a
public random oracle POk . The reader can verify as an exercise that if A and B
use as their key POk evaluated at K,instead of K, then the construction
[ •−−−−
→ , ←
−−−−• , POk ]

π

−→

(σ E •=====• )h

is achieved, for the described protocol π and a suitable simulator σ, where h is
as explained above.
Informally, what is exploited here is that the application of a random oracle
converts an unguessable value into a uniformly random value. This is a general
principle, and the value of a security proof in the ROM is questionable since one
more or less assumes to be true what one cannot prove. Substantial research efforts have recently been devoted to achieving this construction without random
oracle, for a standard hardness assumption such as that for integer factorization.

7.3.3 Impossibility of Implementing a Random Oracle
While the ROM is attractive and allows for simple security proofs, one can show
that a public random oracle cannot be constructed. In other words, there cannot
exist a hash function, no matter how ingeniously designed, which behaves like
a random oracle. More concretely, the following theorem was shown by Canetti
et al.11
11 R. Canetti, O. Goldreich, and S. Halevi, The random oracle methodology, revisited, in Proceedings of the 30th Annual ACM Symposium on the Theory of Computing, pages 209–218. ACM Press, 1998.

142

Theorem 7.10. There exist cryptographic schemes (e.g. a digital signature scheme),
making use of a hash function, which are secure in the ROM (based on some intractability assumption) but which are insecure for every instantiation of POk as a concrete hash
function (or a hash function class with a public parameter).
Proof. The following is only a proof sketch, simplifying the arguments of Canetti
et al. Several technical details would have to be made more precise. The basic
idea of the proof is as follows. Assume there exists a secure signature scheme S̃
(based on some intractability assumption). We now define a signature scheme
S which has available a black-box B that contains either a random oracle or the
implementation of a hash function. The message input to the signature scheme
S is, before signing it with S̃, interpreted as a program of a hash function. If
it is a syntactically correct program taking inputs from {0, 1}∗ and producing
outputs in {0, 1}n, then S tests whether the program’s output and the black-box
B’s output agree, for sufficiently many inputs. The number of test inputs is
chosen such that a random oracle has negligible probability of producing these
outputs for the given inputs. Hence the test is positive if B contains a hash
function and the message is chosen as the program code of the hash function
in B. In contrast, the test is negative (except with negligible probability) if B
contains a random oracle.
The message is signed using the scheme S̃. If the test is positive, then S
appends the secret key of S̃ to the signature, and hence S becomes completely
insecure. In summary, if B contains a random oracle (i.e., in the ROM), then this
is a secure signature scheme (it is essentially identical to S̃), but if B contains
any hash function (with, of course, known program code), then the scheme is
insecure since there exists a message that renders the scheme insecure.
